#5249. Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🗳️ Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Market Structure Bill Resurfaces

Bitcoin spent Tuesday night under $76,000, a seven-week low, with traders bracing for whatever a Democratic Congress meant for crypto. By Wednesday afternoon it was back above $80,800, up 4% on the day.

The easy story is that uncertainty lifted and crypto exhaled. And that could be part of it. But oil helped. WTI fell 9% to $88 a barrel, down almost 17% on the week, as Iran’s parliament speaker said voters had “said NO TO WAR.” The DOW jumped 3% on the day and Nasdaq +1.6% in what was a huge green day across markets.

James Butterfill of CoinShares put it plainly: “For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections.”

But the policy news that landed Wednesday afternoon matters more than the candles.

Eleanor Terrett reported that House Democrats are discussing expanding Maxine Waters’ Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, originally an amendment to the CLARITY Act in 2025, into a full market structure bill next Congress. The draft would fold in Democratic CLARITY provisions that the House rejected last year, plus provisions Senate Democrats and Republicans had already agreed on before the September cloture vote that killed the bill. Three industry sources described the talks to her.

That’s a genuine surprise. Twenty-four hours ago the consensus was that market structure legislation was dead for two years. Waters is the likely incoming chair of House Financial Services, and she is apparently planning to write a bill rather than hold hearings. But the bill will have some changes.

The new proposals under discussion would give the SEC a larger role supervising crypto markets and leave the CFTC with a smaller one. The draft is expected to carry ethics provisions restricting the crypto activities of Trump and other federal officials. Those provisions are what Democrats wanted all along, and what many thought would kill any chances the bill has of getting signed.

So one of the first courses of action from this Democrat-led Congress is to enact some reasonable changes to the Clarity Act to try to get it passed in 2027. That feels like a very bullish first step.

#5247. Ethereum software bug triggers emergency fixes across major layer-2 networks

Paradigm says a bug in Reth could produce incorrect blockchain state calculations, prompting coordinated patches across major Ethereum infrastructure.

A software flaw that could allow specially crafted transactions to produce incorrect blockchain state calculations has prompted coordinated fixes across Ethereum infrastructure.

On Nov. 5, Georgios Konstantopoulos, chief technology officer at crypto investment firm Paradigm, said that developers had released patches for Reth, Optimism, Base and Tempo after discovering an error in software used to process blockchain transactions.

The vulnerability affected Reth, an Ethereum execution client developed by Paradigm that provides critical infrastructure for networks operating within the Ethereum Virtual Machine (EVM) ecosystem.

According to Konstantopoulos, the flaw originated in Reth's legacy state-root calculation system, which could return an incorrect result when processing certain smart contract storage configurations.

A state root is a cryptographic fingerprint representing a blockchain's current state, including account balances and smart contract data. An incorrect calculation can cause nodes to disagree about the validity of a block, potentially disrupting transaction processing or network synchronization.

“Reth's legacy state root code had an edge case,” Konstantopoulos explained, adding that a carefully crafted transaction could trigger the error under specific conditions.

Reth's block-building software used the vulnerable calculation method on lower-end hardware and in networks built with Optimism's OP Stack, extending the potential exposure beyond Ethereum's mainnet infrastructure.

The Tempo development team discovered the issue through automated security testing. The testing technique, known as fuzzing, involves feeding software unusual or unexpected inputs to uncover errors that ordinary testing might miss.

Konstantopoulos said Tempo had also been patched, while Reth developers released version 2.10.0, Optimism published OP Reth v2.8.1, and Base issued version 1.5.3.

The Reth release classified the update as high priority for node operators, including those responsible for building blocks.

The coordinated response highlights a vulnerability in Ethereum's increasingly interconnected software ecosystem: multiple blockchain networks can inherit the same underlying defect when they rely on shared execution software.

However, Konstantopoulos said the affected code did not expose users of Base and other OP Stack networks to faulty withdrawals.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

He explained that these networks' withdrawal systems were not vulnerable to the error, while ecosystem bridges generally rely on transaction receipt roots or multisignature authorization rather than the affected state-root calculations.

He also apologized for the disruption, emphasizing Paradigm's responsibility for maintaining infrastructure used across the Ethereum ecosystem.

Base separately confirmed that the vulnerability had not affected its users, providing additional details about how its security systems prevented the error from threatening the network's integrity.

In a Nov. 5 status update, the Coinbase-backed network said it discovered the Reth issue on Nov. 4 and immediately patched its block builders.

The team also independently recalculated the blockchain's state root to verify its accuracy.

Base said its proof system uses a separate implementation to verify state roots, providing an additional check capable of detecting discrepancies in calculations produced by Reth.

“We've verified the current state root is correct and users were not affected.”

The version 1.5.3 release also addresses a separate operational consequence of the defect, preventing Base validators from halting when encountering invalid state roots.

Node operators were encouraged to upgrade, although Base said they would not need to resynchronize their nodes or take additional recovery steps.

Nevertheless, concerns about the software flaw prompted precautionary measures across parts of the decentralized finance ecosystem.

Frax Finance reported on Nov. 5 that cross-chain messaging protocol LayerZero had temporarily suspended certain transfer routes while developers assessed the situation.

Frax said its assets and smart contracts continued operating normally and that user funds remained safe.

The restrictions did not establish that LayerZero's bridge contracts were vulnerable to the Reth defect. Instead, they demonstrated how concerns about shared blockchain infrastructure can prompt other services to restrict activity while assessing operational risks.

There were no confirmed reports of funds being stolen through the state-root calculation flaw.

Konstantopoulos said the coordinated patching effort had secured Reth-based infrastructure, including systems that depend on blockchain state proofs.

However, the availability of corrected software does not mean every independently operated node has installed it.

The remaining priority is for infrastructure providers and node operators to deploy the patched releases, while services that restricted activity determine when they can resume normal operations.

#5243. Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman has hit back at criticism of his comments about computer memory, saying they were taken out of context and that he has long argued for the world to make more of it.

"I actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context," Altman tweeted early Wednesday. "I have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time."

but only because...i actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context.

i have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time.

The post came as Altman leaned into the backlash. Replying to users joking about his remarks, he said he "lol'd" at one meme and, when another asked him to rename his X account "Ram Altman," replied "ok yeah," briefly changing his display name before explaining he had only done so because he wanted people to have plenty of memory.

Altman's original comments came in an interview with YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, where he discussed shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom. "From like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time," he said. "You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment."

In the same interview, Altman also blamed memory makers for being slow to expand production, and said he would be "very sad" if prices for chips and other components did not fall.

The remarks drew widespread criticism, including from Elon Musk, who mocked Altman on Sunday with a meme of the Inglourious Basterds character Hans Landa asking, "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote.

The spat comes amid a global memory shortage driven by demand from AI data centers. SK hynix, one of the world's largest memory makers, warned last week that demand for memory would outstrip supply through 2030.

Contract prices for 16GB DDR5 memory chips rose from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 in September 2026, an increase of more than 550%, according to Tom's Hardware. On an earnings call this week, Chen Ching-wen, CEO of memory maker Team Group, said there was "no downward price trend whatsoever," and warned that some buyers could face a situation where "even money can't buy goods."

The crunch has already pushed up prices for consumer devices. In April, Meta raised the price of its Quest 3 and Quest 3S headsets by $50 to $100, weeks after Sony increased PlayStation 5 prices.

#5241. Greece ends years of crypto tax uncertainty with new 10% levy on Bitcoin profits

Annual gains up to €500 are exempt under the approved bill, while directives on transparency and audits are still forthcoming.

Greece has approved a 10% cryptocurrency capital gains tax and a separate 15% levy on staking and decentralized finance (DeFi) income.

The legislation, passed by parliament on Nov. 5, establishes the country's first comprehensive framework for taxing digital assets, with the new rules scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027.

According to Reuters, annual cryptocurrency gains of up to €500 will be exempt, while investors exceeding that threshold will face taxation on their profits.

The framework also allows qualifying losses to be carried forward for five years to offset future gains.

Under the finance ministry's October proposal, staking, lending and DeFi liquidity rewards would attract a 15% tax as interest income, separating those earnings from trading profits.

Reuters reports a 10% capital-gains tax with annual gains up to €500 exempt; implementation directives are forthcoming.

The consultation proposed 10% on individuals' transfer gains, generally transfer price minus acquisition price.

The consultation proposed treating staking, lending and liquidity returns as interest at 15%.

Parliament's approval settles the headline framework, while the promised directives address transparency and audit rules.

The ministry also proposed exempting crypto-to-crypto swaps from immediate taxation, although parliamentary concerns about the final wording leave their treatment unresolved.

Meanwhile, the legislation provides a 12-month window for investors to voluntarily declare cryptocurrency gains from previous years without late-filing penalties or interest, subject to specified conditions.

The measure comes as Greek authorities struggle to monitor cryptocurrency activity conducted through overseas platforms.

A senior finance ministry official reportedly said that the government could not estimate potential tax revenue because most domestic investors use foreign exchanges, making the market difficult to measure.

Further directives covering transparency requirements and tax audits are expected in the coming weeks.

Greece's new rate places it among Europe's lower-tax jurisdictions for cryptocurrency investors, even as several countries have increased taxes on digital asset profits.

According to a Nov. 6 analysis by CryptoDiffer, Cyprus taxes crypto gains at 8%, while Belgium and Bulgaria apply 10% rates and Croatia charges 12%.

Other European countries have moved in the opposite direction.

Italy increased its standard cryptocurrency capital-gains tax to 33% in 2026, while Romania raised its rate to 16%. Sweden imposes a 30% capital-gains tax on qualifying crypto profits.

However, some European jurisdictions offer investors exemptions based on how long they hold their assets.

CryptoDiffer identified Germany and Portugal as exempting qualifying cryptocurrency gains after one year of holding, while Luxembourg applies a six-month threshold. Croatia offers an exemption after two years and Czechia after three.

Switzerland, Slovenia and Malta were also identified as jurisdictions where qualifying private-investor crypto gains can be exempt from capital-gains tax.

These differences reflect Europe's fragmented approach to crypto taxation, with investors facing substantially different liabilities depending on their country, holding period and classification of investment activity.

#5236. AI watchdog report indicates we may have less than 6 months before AI super intelligence arrives

The estimate covers well-specified tasks; METR's judgment that AI drives most of the acceleration rests partly on redacted evidence.

Anthropic's AI models were likely improving on tasks with clear success criteria roughly twice as fast in October 2026 as they did between March 2024 and October 2025, according to a Nov. 5 assessment by METR, an independent model-evaluation group.

METR judges that AI assistance probably accounts for most of the recent acceleration. But Anthropic redacted much of the evidence behind that judgment, leaving outsiders with a clearer view of the faster benchmark trend than of what caused it.

The central published measurement uses AECI, Anthropic's version of the Epoch Capabilities Index. METR estimated that AECI's fitted growth rate after October 2025 was 1.9 times its earlier rate, with a 95% confidence interval of 1.5 to 2.8 times. The analysis includes snapshots of unreleased models, so it covers more than the products customers could access.

METR interprets that trend as roughly two years of historical benchmark progress achieved in the past year. The 1.9-times fit is distinct from its broader judgment of roughly twice the historical pace on well-specified tasks. That judgment carries substantial uncertainty, including whether the same acceleration extends to broader work.

Those limits matter because success on clean, easily graded tasks may translate unevenly into real-world usefulness. METR also cautions that an unusually slow historical baseline could make the acceleration appear larger. In the other direction, model results arrive after training, potentially concealing a more recent change in research speed.

METR says it confidently ruled out an acceleration in the growth of training compute as the explanation for the faster progress. The detailed evidence supporting that finding was redacted.

Beyond that exclusion, separating causes was less decisive. METR says non-AI factors likely played a significant role in the acceleration in late 2025, while judging that AI assistance now accounts for a majority of it.

The distinction also leaves people at the center of the most important training work. METR says AI was not yet making highly significant, fully autonomous contributions to those parts of Anthropic's model-training process. The reported speedup primarily involved researchers using AI assistance.

AECI measures benchmark progress on well-specified tasks. The 1.9x fit is separate from METR's broader roughly 2x judgment. Attribution to AI is substantially uncertain, and the diagram does not quantify the shares of different causes.

Anthropic withheld a majority of the evidence METR found most informative for its risk conclusions. The redactions covered detailed estimates of acceleration and AI's contribution at different training stages, intermediate capability measurements, algorithmic-progress estimates, R&D inputs, bottleneck analyses and forecasts. Some interview and AI-contribution evidence was published, leaving readers able to inspect only part of the case behind METR's judgment.

Anthropic co-founder Sam McCandlish broadly agreed that AI was speeding research, according to The Associated Press. AP reported that he acknowledged the redactions made METR's work harder to check, while defending them because the details were commercially sensitive and could help other developers accelerate.

METR's companion review of Anthropic's August Risk Report, also published Nov. 5, addresses models available on July 15. It questions the confidence of the public report's argument that an early-warning acceleration threshold had not been crossed. That threshold concerns AI-driven research acceleration as a warning signal, rather than proof that catastrophic risks have materialized.

Researchers at both organizations still leaned toward the threshold remaining uncrossed then, while METR considered a crossing plausible. METR also agreed that direct catastrophic risks from those July models were low.

The main assessment ran from Aug. 12 to Oct. 29 and was separate from the employee-like evaluator program Anthropic announced Oct. 15. For public scrutiny, the unresolved gap is specific: the faster benchmark trend is described, but much of the evidence dividing that progress between AI assistance and other causes remains confidential.

At the pace METR measured, six months could deliver roughly a year’s worth of progress on Anthropic’s benchmarks. The case for superintelligence within that window hinges on what the next models can do with those gains. If they can design training experiments, spot promising results, and help turn them into better models, each generation could shorten the time it takes to build the next.

For that to produce superintelligence within six months, the gains would have to carry over from tasks with clear answers to open-ended work where even experts have to figure out what to try next. The first sign might come from inside an AI lab, when a model runs an experiment its researchers hadn’t thought to try, and the result helps build a more capable successor.

#5234. Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Plunge, $428 Million in Crypto Longs Get Wiped Out

Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Plunge, $428 Million in Crypto Longs Get Wiped Out

Bitcoin erased Wednesday's post-election rebound Thursday as a technology stock selloff spread to cryptocurrencies, triggering hundreds of millions in forced liquidations.

Bitcoin fell to about $76,500, down nearly 5% and back near levels seen before Wednesday's jump above $80,000. Ethereum shed more than 5%, while other major tokens suffered comparable losses. Roughly 90% of the top 100 cryptocurrencies were in the red during the selloff, while roughly $428 million in crypto longs were liquidated in one hour.

Wall Street offered no shelter. The S&P 500 lost about 2.6% in late-morning trading, while the Nasdaq sank nearly 4%, approaching a correction, or a 10% drop from its peak.

The election is part of the answer, but anxiety over AI spending and interest rates is making the damage worse.

Democrats' sweep of Congress initially rattled investors expecting tougher oversight of industries favored by President Donald Trump. Stocks rallied Wednesday as traders bet gridlock would limit major policy changes and falling oil prices would ease inflation. Bitwise Europe analyst André Dragosch also told Decrypt the Democratic sweep was "largely priced in."

Thursday brought a different interpretation. Investors weighed what Democratic control might mean for the data-center boom, particularly in states where voters oppose rising electricity costs and new facilities.

And the new political configuration is already moving things. Voters in at least 29 U.S. jurisdictions backed restrictions on data-center development, although not every measure is binding. UBS estimates potential pauses could threaten 30% of planned U.S. data-center capacity additions in 2028, per Reuters.

"We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout," Swissquote senior analyst Ipek Ozkardeskaya told Reuters.

Chipmakers and AI infrastructure companies took the hit first. The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index dropped about 8%, while CoreWeave and Nebius fell by double digits. Tesla also tumbled roughly 9.5%, extending losses after Elon Musk spent heavily backing Republican candidates. Investors were also questioning the returns on massive AI investments.

The S&P 500 had the biggest bearish intraday movement in months, dropping almost 4% in minutes.

"The market's moving from the promise of AI to the price of AI," Ted Parkhill, chief executive of Incline Investment Management, told Reuters.

The political shift also threatens crypto's ambitions in Washington. Democratic control complicates efforts to revive the stalled Clarity Act, a proposed federal rulebook for digital assets. The new Congress will not take office until January. Politics alone cannot explain Bitcoin losing thousands of dollars in an hour.

Wednesday's rally looked dramatic, but much of its buying pressure came from traders closing losing bets against Bitcoin. About $378 million in short positions were liquidated in one hour as Bitcoin pushed above $80,000, according to CoinGlass data.

Today’s reversal flipped the liquidation machinery in the opposite direction. Exchanges automatically close underfunded longs as prices fall, generating more selling and the enormous red candles visible on Bitcoin's hourly chart.

Crypto was already vulnerable before the election. The market had lost roughly $300 billion in value over the previous month, amid concerns about AI spending, rising borrowing costs and the fallout from last week's multibillion-dollar Binance hack.

Traders also expect another Federal Reserve rate increase in December as inflation persists. Friday's October jobs report could alter those bets; higher rates generally hurt demand for speculative investments.

Technically, Bitcoin's still-forming daily candle is threatening a bearish engulfing pattern, potentially swallowing Wednesday's green body and erasing the breakout in a single session.

The hourly chart shows selling through $79,000 to near $76,100. The next test is $75,000, while reclaiming $80,000 could ease the pressure—but Thursday's candle has not closed, so the pattern remains unconfirmed.

#5230. Ardoino’s 24/7 asset plan depends on buyers willing to wait for cash

Hadron’s RedStone agreement relies on approved buyers to finance fast settlement, while near-instant voluntary holder exits remain on the roadmap.

Tether’s plan to make tokenized assets liquid around the clock relies on buyers willing to advance cash before the underlying investment pays out. Its November 5 agreement with data and settlement provider RedStone gives issuers on Hadron, Tether’s tokenization platform, access to price feeds, net asset value data and an auction-based settlement service for eligible assets. Tether announcement

Paolo Ardoino, Tether’s CEO, framed the collaboration around accurate pricing and continuous liquidity. RedStone’s settlement service, Settle, proposes to bridge the timing gap by having approved liquidity providers take over positions, pay immediately and collect through the issuer’s ordinary redemption process. The buyer finances the wait, making its willingness to submit an auction offer central to faster exits. RedStone’s announcement places near-instant voluntary holder redemptions on the roadmap. RedStone’s Hadron announcement

Hadron’s earlier rollout focused on issuing and managing tokenized assets with compliance tools, as CryptoSlate reported in November 2024. This agreement adds access to valuation and settlement infrastructure that could make those assets more useful as lending collateral. Earlier Hadron coverage

A lender liquidating collateral needs funds to close a borrowing position. A tokenized investment can carry a redemption timetable that extends beyond the moment the lender needs payment. Settle connects that position with approved buyers, also called solvers, that can supply liquid funds and acquire the tokens.

In the model described in RedStone’s April product explanation, a solver buys at a discount and seeks to earn the spread to face value at maturity. Actual redemption proceeds depend on the asset’s terms. The buyer’s financing costs and expected payout shape the price it can offer for taking over the redemption wait. Settle’s funding model

RedStone lists several possible funding strategies, including liquid restaking capital, on-demand stablecoin minting, institutional over-the-counter desks and dedicated settlement vaults. The Hadron announcement leaves the committed capacity of its prospective liquidity providers unspecified.

For lenders, a successful auction turns collateral into repayment funds immediately. For the winning buyer, it creates an investment that still has to complete the issuer’s redemption process. The discount is therefore part of the cost of delivering speed to the exiting side of the trade.

In Settle’s described liquidation model, approved buyers advance immediate funds and collect later through the asset issuer.

Approved buyers accept the redemption wait in exchange for a discounted purchase.

A mechanism illustration. Hadron-specific volumes and committed liquidity are undisclosed. Near-instant voluntary holder exits are on the November announcement’s roadmap.

Qualitative flow diagram of the source-described liquidation sequence. Arrows show direction, with timing labels rather than a scaled duration. Purchase spread is gross and eventual proceeds depend on asset terms.

A mechanism illustration. Hadron-specific volumes and committed liquidity are undisclosed. Near-instant voluntary holder exits are on the November announcement’s roadmap.

Independent documentation from Centrifuge illustrates the distinction. Its investor guide says redemption requests are processed at the manager’s cadence, with the final amount determined by the processing price. Freely transferable tokens can instead be sold at market prices where secondary liquidity is available. Centrifuge investor guide

RedStone describes a proposed voluntary-exit route in which holders set their maximum acceptable haircut and providers quote to buy the position. Large positions can be divided among several providers. RedStone’s Hadron announcement

The same announcement describes near-instant unwinding of leveraged, looped positions as a design capability. In such a position, an investor borrows against tokens, buys more tokens and posts those as collateral again. The release provides no Hadron-specific activation schedule for that unwind feature.

Participation also has boundaries. Providers complete RedStone’s business verification and must satisfy the issuer’s holder requirements, including identity checks or whitelisting. The announcement’s access disclosure excludes persons located, resident or incorporated in the United States.

Settle’s architecture selects the highest valid offer and executes the price update, solver callback, loan repayment and collateral release in one transaction. A failure in that sequence causes the transaction to revert in full. Settle architecture

Buyer availability determines whether that exchange can happen on acceptable terms. In its September explanation of Settle for the HYB tokenized bond fund, RedStone says an auction receiving no offers reverts and liquidation proceeds through the regular route. HYB settlement example

For Hadron issuers, the reviewed announcements and architecture disclose no committed liquidity amount, guaranteed auction coverage or executed settlement volume.

If eligible buyers lack available capital or demand a larger discount than an exiting holder accepts, fast settlement can become harder to obtain. The practical test for Ardoino’s round-the-clock ambition is whether the rollout attracts approved providers willing to offer prices that lenders and eligible holders can use.

#5227. NHTSA proposes cyan lights for robotaxis, following Mercedes/China

The NHTSA has proposed using cyan-colored lights to identify vehicles operating autonomously, along with other additions to get ready for autonomous vehicles.

As the autonomous vehicles start to spread on US roads, questions have come up about how to deal with these new driverless vehicles.

One question involves emergency response – not only do the vehicles need to be prepared to respond to emergency personnel, those personnel need to recognize when a car is operating autonomously. A police officer needs to know that nobody’s in the driver seat, or an emergency responder needs to know that if there’s nobody in the driver’s seat, that’s not necessarily because there’s a missing driver somewhere who needs rescue.

These are just some of the reasons one might want to know whether a car is operating autonomously, but as of yet, there is no standardized messaging to suggest that a vehicle is doing so.

This week, NHTSA proposed an answer. In a draft proposal released Friday, it suggested a whole new color for vehicle lamps, cyan, to signal autonomous operation.

The proposal was actually first put forward by Mercedes, alongside its DRIVE PILOT level 3 driver assist system. This is the first true hands-free system offered in the US, which allows drivers to forgo responsibility for the car under certain conditions, and let the car truly drive itself. This differs from systems like Tesla’s Full Self-Driving, which is actually only a “level 2” system, where the driver is still responsible when the system is on.

We tried Mercedes’ system out in Santa Monica traffic, and were impressed at how quickly we adjusted to our temporary robotic chauffer.

When we saw that system, we also saw Mercedes’ proposal for lighting: cyan lights around the exterior of the vehicle, denoting autonomous operation.

Unfortunately our demo was on a bright sunny day (a necessity to operate the level 3 system, which doesn’t run at night or in bad weather), so the lights didn’t pop as much as they might have in lower light, but you get the drift.

Mercedes trialed the system in California for two years, and it seems the NHTSA is happy with the results, since it is proposing to take the idea national (after input from the SAE, which also likes the idea of using cyan).

The new draft regulation would allow for optional inclusion of cyan lights, but would not require them. So, it’s entirely possible that automakers would decide to save the small cost that it would take to engineer and include these lights.

But by putting the idea out into the regulations, it gets everyone thinking about a future where this color might be required (and I could see that requirement coming quite quickly) – so automakers are likely to start thinking of where these lights would go, and including them, since LEDs don’t really cost anything anyway.

The regulation states that the lights should turn on when the car is operating autonomously, and turn off when it isn’t – they should never be on when a driver is actively driving the vehicle. They should all turn on and off simultaneously, and they should be visible from any angle, with markers on the front, sides and rear, all at least 15 inches off the ground.

China just announced essentially the same regulation earlier this week. The country released a draft standard that would take effect in 2028, is optional, and uses turquoise as its proposed color. This, too, was a reaction to the proliferation of lights on autonomous vehicles in China, and the government has asked automakers to stop doing their own thing and instead follow the new standardized system.

The NHTSA’s draft regulation covers several other aspects of autonomous vehicle operation, mostly by updating Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards (FMVSS) rules to allow some flexibility for certain operations that won’t be used by autonomous vehicles.

For example, cars are required to have a turn signal that turns off automatically after the steering wheel turns beyond a certain threshold and then returns back to neutral. That wouldn’t apply to cars without steering wheels, so the updated rules delete that requirement for autonomous cars. It also changes certain headlight requirements for more design flexibility.

While I’ve got a lot to say about the corrupt reality TV character running the DoT, and how he’s trying to raise your energy costs and kill children on bicycles, this autonomous vehicle regulation is a long time coming and is needed.

There are still some careerists in the NHTSA who take their job seriously, and are pushing regulations like this, and we can be thankful that not all of them have been forced out by the rampant corruption the US is seeing right now.

That said, the loosening of rules for autonomous vehicles is something we need to keep our eye on. Certain things, like allowing reasonable changes to FMVSS rules for things that really make no sense for an AV (like the turn signal change, or various required driver displays), are totally reasonable.

But it’s also hard not to look at this and say, well, could the guy who has given hundreds of millions in bribes to try to cancel investigations against him and wants to sell you an unusable car without a steering wheel be behind any of this?

NHTSA would be better off just shutting down and copy-paste regulations from EU on vehicle safety and China on autonomy. Both require more stringent safety and it would align the US with the larger markets.

Sadly, the US regulators are no longer independent thanks to the rampant political corruption and they are no longer sufficiently staffed to develop and implement regulations. Outsource development and become an agency that enforces the regulations developed in the more advanced markets.

After all, that steering-wheel-less car was subject to an immediate NHTSA investigation when it was suddenly put into service without forewarning, with the NHTSA pointing out myriad problems. Changing the rules after the fact to accompany a vehicle that already violated them doesn’t set great precedent.

So… we can be happy for the adaptation to new technology, but still need to ensure that safety is the North Star, rather than Mr. Musk’s fantasies. We have no specific reason in mind to oppose this specific regulation, but an environment so rife with corruption does not foster trust with the general populace, so here we are.

The rule will soon be posted for public comment, for a 30 day period, under docket NHTSA-2026-1519 at www.regulations.gov.

#5224. Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

Democrats to Push Broader Crypto Market Structure Bill After Sweeping US Midterms: Report

House Democrats are reportedly set to progress crypto legislation after sweeping the U.S. midterms.

There are plans to expand the Crypto Investor Consumer Bill of Rights—a Clarity Act amendment led by Dem. Maxine Waters—into a broader market structure bill, journalist Eleanor Terrett tweeted. The proposal would include giving the SEC more power to supervise the crypto markets and limiting the role of the CFTC, sources told Terrett.

🚨SCOOP: House Democrats are discussing plans to expand the Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, @RepMaxineWaters’ amendment to the Clarity Act in 2025, into a broader crypto “market structure” bill next Congress.

The proposal would fold in Democratic Clarity Act…

— Eleanor Terrett (@EleanorTerrett) November 4, 2026

On the surface, the midterm election results could be seen as a major hit to crypto policy in the U.S., with President Trump's Republican administration widely regarded as the pro-crypto party, while the Democrats were deemed more hostile. However, experts, told Decrypt, the tide might be turning and crypto policy evolving into a more bipartisan issue.

Advocacy organization Stand With Crypto endorsed 45 candidates in the midterms, per its election tracker, with 27 pro-crypto candidate wins, 12 losses, and six races still live at the time of writing. Nearly half of the endorsed candidates were Democrats.

"We’re proud to see a majority of our endorsed candidates win their elections," Mason Lynaugh, executive director of Stand With Crypto, said in a statement shared with Decrypt. "To the candidates who won’t return to Washington, thank you for going to bat for crypto-owning Americans when it counted."

Congratulations to the pro-crypto champions who won their elections, including more than 50 pro-crypto new members heading to Congress.

In the closest races, our advocates showed up: in Florida's 9th District, Darren Soto won by 1,477 votes. In that same district, we mobilized… pic.twitter.com/lcq055GNET

— Stand With Crypto🛡️ (@standwithcrypto) November 4, 2026

James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares, described Republican Bryan Steil as a "meaningful loss" for crypto. The Wisconsin Congressman was the chair of the House digital assets subcommittee and closely involved in efforts to advance the Clarity Act—which were stymied when it stalled in the Senate earlier this year.

That said, Butterfill also highlighted several winners—Democrats Chris Pappas in New Hampshire and James Talarico in Texas, as well as Wyoming Republican Harriet Hageman—who have supported crypto policy. He stressed, however, that the challenge is now "converting" that support into passing legislation. Some believe that could slow an already difficult path for the Clarity Act.

"I wouldn’t say it’s game over for Clarity, but passing it has become tougher," Carlos Guzman, vice president of research at crypto trading firm GSR, told Decrypt. "Some Democrats support clearer crypto rules, but I wouldn’t expect it to be high on their agenda. Any agreement will likely require stronger provisions on things like consumer protection and conflicts of interest."

The Clarity Act looks to establish rules for crypto markets and has been batted back and forth since 2025. Democrats have pushed back on the bill, citing Trump's crypto conflicts of interest, with Senator Elizabeth Warren (D-MA) pushing for the bill to ban officials and their families from profiting from the sector.

"For other crypto policies, the election doesn’t automatically replace the leadership of the SEC or CFTC or stop their rulemaking," Guzman said. "Congressional oversight does change, however, so we might see more scrutiny of regulatory decisions, with Democrats also able to influence agency funding and future appointments."

Butterfill added that he believes crypto is becoming less dependent on the Clarity Act, as the SEC and CFTC push for regulatory clarity without the need for Congress. He also said that AI regulation may take eyes away from crypto, slowing crypto policy in the process.

Following the midterms, Bitcoin has exhibited volatility, and is currently trading at around $76,820, down 4.1% on the day, per Decrypt data.

"The result alone would not make me change my medium-term Bitcoin outlook," Butterfill told Decrypt. "Slower reform could delay some institutional activity, but we would expect the more direct impact to fall on US crypto businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. We would revisit the outlook if political uncertainty materially weakened investment flows or tightened financial conditions."

#5222. Chinese EVs found back door into Western car markets – at 40% off MSRP

The dumb oil war in Iran is driving up the cost of just about everything, including new cars. That’s giving Chinese EVs a huge advantage over Western “legacy” car brands already lagging in tech and struggling with affordability – and those legacy brands about to have a whole new to them problem on their hands: USED Chinese EVs.

Western automakers have leaned on protectionist trade policies to keep cheaper Chinese rivals at bay, placing strict limits on the number of new Chinese cars allowed in and hefty tariffs on new battery-electric vehicles (BEVs) are as high as 45.3% in the EU. That supply limitation is, of course, ratcheting up demand for the high-tech, low-cost Chinese machines, and buyers in Europe and Canada are finding that used Chinese EVs – which offer comparable tech to the newest Western EVs, a price tag up to 40% lower than their already low MSRPs, and which are not subject to the same protectionist import duties – are a workable alternative.

According to data shared by importers/exporters China EV Marketplace (CEVM), pre-owned vehicle sales to EU customers grew 280% year over year, with sales climbing to 7,900 units through September.

“Tariffs have already weakened demand for new Chinese BEVs among our European customers. We expect the hybrid import cap to put pressure on new PHEV sales too,” CEVM COO Jakub Geršl told CarNewsChina. “Pre-owned cars are exempt from the additional BEV tariffs and from the new hybrid (PHEV) quota. That is why we are making pre-owned vehicles the main focus of our European business.”

CEVM has more than 2,600 used Chinese EVs on its website as I type this, and their inventory is refreshed almost daily with fresh pre-owned examples of cars from brands like BYD, Geely, Zeekr, and others – and the company claims its prices are about 40% below the original price (MSRP) on average.

“Lower new-car prices put pressure on pre-owned prices as well,” Geršl adds. “Our Shanghai-based team searches for those opportunities. We regularly find cars with around 6,000 km on the clock priced 40% below their original Chinese list price.”

That price reduction is driven by a number of factors, including the relentless march of technological progress, the flood of new and updated EVs coming out of Chinese car brands, and a burgeoning domestic Chinese car culture that values newness above all else. If you’re reading between the lines there, I think you’ll find that that means more low-mileage, low-price, high-tech, and high-value Chinese EVs are coming to the West – and probably a lot sooner than brands like VW, who are struggling to compete as it is, would want.

And that’s the real problem – whether you agree with him or not, Elon Musk will tell you that technology and markets move faster than legislation, and once consumers go electric, precious few of them ever go back to gas or diesel, regardless of what that dude filming himself wearing PitVipers in the drivers’ seat of his pickup and your racist uncle on Facebook are telling you.

My concern would be lack of service facilities. At some point every car, even EVs, needs to go to the service center for something, and the more technology-laden the vehicle, the more important it is to have service centers that know what they're doing. I wouldn't count on the local Chevy dealer being able to deal with a mandarin software stack.

Let us know if you agree, and if you’d give a used Chinese EV a chance if one showed up at your local Carvana, in the comments.

#5219. Anchorage Digital seeks CFTC swap dealer registration for crypto options business

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The firm plans to offer European-style bitcoin and ether options with maturities of up to one year if its application is approved.

Institutional crypto platform Anchorage Digital has filed an application to register a swap dealer with the Commodity Futures Trading Commission as it looks to expand its institutional crypto options business.

The application was submitted to the National Futures Association through Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer, LLC, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

Anchorage is now awaiting registration while the NFA reviews its application. CEO and co-founder Nathan McCauley offered no estimate of when that process might finish, but said the company would share further updates as appropriate. "We aren't in a position to speculate on timing," he told The Block.

If the application is approved, McCauley said Anchorage Digital Swap Dealer expects to offer European-style options on bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) and ether Ethereum (ETH), with maturities ranging up to one year. The firm intends to add more products later, he added, subject to its own approval process and regulatory requirements.

McCauley said that once registration is complete, institutional clients would be able to trade digital asset derivatives with a CFTC-registered counterparty alongside use of Anchorage Digital's existing custody and other services.

Much of Anchorage's existing options business growth has come from public companies holding bitcoin and ether on their balance sheets, the firm said in the statement, while asset managers and funds using options to manage their exposure have also contributed. McCauley would not disclose specific trading volumes or growth figures when asked by The Block, and declined to explain how Anchorage had previously arranged options trades for clients.

"What we can say is that institutional demand for regulated access to digital asset derivatives continues to grow, and establishing a dedicated swap dealer entity is part of how we're building the infrastructure to meet it," he said.

Anchorage Digital Bank, N.A. became the first federally chartered digital asset bank in 2021 and is regulated by the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency. The swap dealer entity would face CFTC requirements for capital, risk management, and margin, using the same framework the Dodd-Frank Act applied to the banks for interest rate, credit, and FX markets, the firm said.

"It will bring our options business under the same federal framework as the dealers they already face," McCauley said in the statement.

In March, Anchorage Digital added collateral management to its Atlas platform, including 24/7 monitoring and automated margin calls for loans, derivatives, and other institutional transactions, The Block reported at the time. It also acquired Securitize's wealth management unit in December to expand its federally regulated crypto services for registered investment advisors.

An Injective affiliate recently became an SEC-registered transfer agent amid an expanding tokenization push.

Canary Capital has launched the Canary Staked INJ ETF, offering investors exposure to Injective's native INJ token. The fund will trade on the Nasdaq under the ticker INJC.

The ETF seeks to earn additional INJ through in Injective's proof-of-stake validation process, with net staking rewards reflected in its net asset value.

#5214. Volkswagen’s new electric Jetta offers 285 miles of range for under $12K

Volkswagen is offering its Chinese-market Jetta M6 electric sedan with nearly 290 miles of CLTC-rated range for less than $12,000 (USD), and if that’s not what “der people’s wagen” should be all about, I don’t know what is.

Launched as a joint venture between FAW and Volkswagen Group, the Jetta brand’s first battery electric car is finally here – and it is a genuinely accessible, capable, and kinda decent-looking family sedan in the same way VW’s original Type I was when it was first developed in the 1940s.

The new Jetta M6 was launched earlier this in three variants: the 465 Pro, 555 Max and 555 Ultra; each priced at 79,800 yuan (~$11,800), 89,800 yuan and 99,800 yuan, respectively, with the model numbers corresponding to the car’s estimated CLTC range in kilometers (which maths out around ~285 and 345 miles, if you’re curious).

Built on FAW’s Jiesi architecture, the M6 measures 4,806 mm long and 1,868 mm wide, putting it right in that sedan sweet spot between the Civic/Corolla and Accord/Camry crews, and within a few mm this way and that of the Tesla Model 3, so it should have plenty of room for young professionals and families – but the M6′ real selling point is its tech.

Inside, the Jetta M6 offers an 8.88″ driver display along with a big 15.6″ central touchscreen that integrates Chinese AI assistants Doubao and DeepSeek and ships with Apple CarPlay baked in. Advanced ADAS with, “intelligent cruise assistance, automatic emergency braking, and automated parking” is standard.

The entry-level “465 Pro” version of the Jetta M6 comes equipped with 42.9 kWh LFP battery and a 113 kW (~150 hp) electric motor driving the front wheels, a combination the company says is good for a 8.3-second sprint to 100 km/h (62 mph). Stepping up to the 555 versions gets you a larger 51.9 kWh battery and more powerful 145 kW (~195 hp) motor that cuts that sprint to 6.8 seconds.

Both the 42.9 and 51.0 kWh batteries are lithium iron phosphate deals supplied by CALB, with charging from 20% to 80% taking about 30 minutes.

There are parts of the US where a car like this would be perfect -- it's got enough range for the daily commute and short weekend day trips. Not for everyone, but for enough people that it would be a big deal.

Would those features and performance numbers be enough to get you to look at something like this in the US, even at twice the price? It seems to me that North America’s VW dealers need all the help they can get, and a volume leader like this with a “back to basics” ad campaign that plays on a different kind of nostalgia – nostalgia for a time when people could afford a Jetta, maybe? – could be a winner. Let us know what you think in the comments.

#5213. Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs’ Leap Launches Mobile App for Trading Tokenized Stocks and Crypto

Solana Labs is betting the network’s next wave of traders wants more than meme coins.

Leap, a new company under Solana Labs, launched a beta of its mobile trading app Thursday, letting users trade tokenized stocks, ETFs, metals, currencies, and crypto in one place, around the clock and subject to restrictions.

The app is available in early access on iPhone through Apple’s public TestFlight program, with an Android version coming soon. It isn’t available in the U.K. or other restricted regions.

Leap is built by the team behind Dialect, which Solana Labs acquired earlier this year. Dialect developed Solana Actions and Blinks, a protocol that turns blockchain transactions into shareable links, which launched in 2024, as well as the cashtags feature on X.

The company is pitching the app as an alternative to fast-paced meme coin trading, aimed at people who want to make better-informed decisions over the long term. Its research team curates market-moving news and pins each story to the price chart at the moment it broke.

The app tracks profit and loss across every deposit, withdrawal, trade and fee, including each position’s breakeven price. Users enter an amount in dollars, and each trade executes as an onchain swap.

Leap uses Jupiter for trade routing, Helius for infrastructure and Birdeye for market data.

“The Solana blockchain now confirms a trade in about 150 milliseconds thanks to Alpenglow, so the network is now faster than ever,” said Chris Osborn, Leap’s general manager and Dialect’s founder. Alpenglow a consensus overhaul designed to sharply cut Solana’s confirmation times.

“Every app that brings new people onchain makes the whole ecosystem stronger,” said Solana Labs co-founder and CEO Anatoly Yakovenko.

Leap isn’t charging its own fees at launch, though network and routing costs apply. It plans to use future fees to buy and burn SOL, permanently removing tokens from circulation, and to fund a referral program. Early waitlist members, dubbed the Founders Club, would earn 30% of the trading fees generated by users they invite and 5% from those users’ own referrals.

Leap arrives as tokenized stocks gain traction on Solana and beyond. Securitize has brought tokenized Nvidia, Apple, and Amazon shares to Solana. The trend has also met pushback: 56 companies have objected to having their shares traded on OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

#5211. Base deploys Denim upgrade on mainnet, replacing Flashblocks with 200ms block times

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Denim retires Flashblocks, adds millisecond-resolution timestamps, and updates B20 transfer and compliance controls.

Base's fourth mainnet upgrade, Denim, is live, with the Coinbase-incubated Layer 2 now producing a full canonical block every 200 milliseconds, or five per second, the project said in a statement shared with The Block on Thursday.

The upgrade also introduces millisecond-resolution timestamps for smart contracts, alongside new transfer protections and compliance controls for tokens using Base's B20 standard.

Base said the upgrade retires Flashblocks and changes how the pending block tag works, making block production "simpler, more consistent, and more reliable." Each block now follows its parent exactly 200 milliseconds later, with the protocol enforcing the interval rather than relying on the sequencer's clock.

Applications that previously relied on Flashblocks must migrate to standard canonical block subscriptions, according to the statement.

"Base’s north star is to bring the world onchain, starting with trading, financing, and payments," the statement read. "Those use cases need a chain that updates state often and lets builders reason about time precisely."

According to the statement, the upgrade also introduces BaseTime, which gives smart contracts access to Unix timestamps in milliseconds. Ethereum-compatible block.timestamp remains unchanged at whole-second resolution, while new RPC fields provide millisecond timestamps for blocks, transactions, and logs.

Denim also introduces reusable compliance policies through a NOT operator and extends transfer-executor restrictions to direct transfers. It also blocks transfers, mints and seizures that send tokens to the token contract's own address, Base said.

The upgrade follows Azul, Beryl and Cobalt, Base's first three independent upgrades since its 2023 launch. Azul introduced a new client stack and multiproof system, while Beryl brought the B20 token standard. Cobalt added conditional transactions and expanded B20 compliance features.

#5209. Argentine Bitcoiners Debut “The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto” Artwork in Downtown Buenos Aires

“The Genesis of Satoshi Nakamoto,” a 2.4-meter, multi-dimensional cube, was presented on October 29 at Polo Dot as part of the Open Fest of LABITCONF 2026. Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the Premio B·Arte, the work combines art, technology, and education in a journey through Bitcoin’s fundamental principles. The inauguration featured guided remarks by Rodolfo Andragnes and a musical performance by Mayumaná, according to an announcement by the organization.

Buenos Aires now has a monument dedicated to Satoshi Nakamoto, the person—or group of people—behind the creation of Bitcoin, whose identity remains unknown.

Driven by Bitcoin.ar through the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte, the monument was conceived not only as a tribute to Satoshi, but also as an artistic and educational experience. Through its five faces—four lateral and one upper—it proposes a journey to approach different concepts that explain Bitcoin’s origin, functioning, and philosophy.

During the inauguration, Rodolfo Andragnes — co-founder of Bitcoin.ar and LABITCONF — accompanied the public with words and explanations about the details of the work, guiding the reading of its various elements and the meaning behind each representation. The presentation was accompanied by a musical show by Mayumaná, which added rhythm and sound to the opening of this new space dedicated to Bitcoin’s history.

Each of the monument’s faces offers a gateway to a different aspect of Bitcoin. The figure of Satoshi Nakamoto, for example, represents the anonymous character of its creator and the collective dimension of a technology that, once set in motion, continued to grow thanks to thousands of people distributed around the world. The reflections and multiplied geometric forms also evoke the structure of a network that replicates and expands.

Another of its faces addresses Bitcoin’s programmed scarcity through a cube that successively divides into halves in a Fibonacci sequence. The representation refers to “the halving”, the mechanism that approximately every four years halves the reward miners receive for validating blocks and that regulates the issuance of new bitcoins. Starting at 50 bitcoins per block, the total reward gets cut in half every four years for roughly 130 years, until 21 million bitcoins have been emitted and the block reward becomes zero. This supply limit is iconic to the cryptocurrency and key to its qualities as hard money.

The work also incorporates the first words of Bitcoin’s foundational document, the white paper published by Satoshi Nakamoto in October 31st,  2008. There, the possibility of making electronic payments directly between people, without needing to go through a financial institution, is proposed. That idea constitutes a foundational principle of the technology.

Another face reproduces information from the genesis block, the first block of the Bitcoin chain, created on January 3, 2009. Among its elements appears the reference to the headline from The Times newspaper that Satoshi incorporated into that block, a historical mark that places Bitcoin’s birth in its context and anchors it to history and time.

Thus, the monument proposes something more than contemplating a sculpture: it invites one to walk around it, observe it from different angles, and discover the concepts behind each of its parts; “The intention is to bring Bitcoin closer to those who are already part of its community, but also to awaken the curiosity of those who do not yet know its history.”

The monument was created by Ricardo Alonso, engineer, and Celeste Difabio Videla, architect, a couple of artists from Bariloche who won the fifth edition of the Premio B·Arte. The work measures 2.40 meters in width, length, and height, and is made of stainless steel and glass.

Its construction involved teams and collaborators from different localities across the country, in a process that combined different trades, materials, and techniques. Aída Pippo, visual artist, researcher of new technologies applied to art, and member of the Board of Directors of Bitcoin.ar, led the project and coordinated the different stages of production and assembly.

“It gives me great pride to feel that this is something we can leave in our city and in our country,” Andragnes expressed during the presentation, highlighting the value of a collective initiative that seeks to leave a cultural and educational footprint.

For their part, the creators noted that they sought for the work to function as a meeting point for the community and, at the same time, as a gateway for those who do not yet know Bitcoin. “You don’t always have the possibility of leaving something for posterity,” they shared when speaking of the experience of seeing the monument finished and installed in Buenos Aires.

With this inauguration, Bitcoin.ar seeks for the monument to become a permanent resource for the city: a space that schools, universities, families, and visitors can approach to learn Bitcoin’s history, discover its principles, and open new conversations about the future of money and technology. The intention is that what is presented today as an artistic work can continue to grow through educational activities and new experiences, and remain in Buenos Aires as a legacy for future generations.

The project was financed by the Fundación Bitcoin Iberoamérica and represents a new way of bringing Bitcoin’s history and principles to public and cultural spaces, beyond screens and specialized environments.

#5208. Police pull over two men riding an electric couch down the street

Electric scooters have carried just about everything at this point, from commuters and tourists to groceries and probably more than a few ill-advised passengers. But two riders in Charlotte, North Carolina, may have just raised the bar after police stopped them cruising down the street on an actual couch apparently powered by a pair of Lime electric scooters.

Officers with the Charlotte-Mecklenburg Police Department encountered the unusual contraption traveling down South Tryon Street, according to a recent post shared by the department.

“Well, this was certainly a first,” police wrote. “Our EDU Officers encountered quite the situation this weekend when they stopped two gentlemen traveling down S. Tryon Street on a couch… Yes, an actual couch.”

From the photo, the setup appears to consist of a full-size sofa perched across two Lime shared electric scooters. Exactly how the scooters were attached – or how steering was accomplished – isn’t clear. But judging by the fact that the riders apparently made it far enough to attract police attention, the system seems to have worked at least reasonably well with some coordinated efforts by the dual pilots.

And frankly, as someone who has famously seen and operated plenty of bizarre electric vehicles over the years, I have to give some points for creativity here.

Police seemed to feel similarly, though they were understandably less enthusiastic about the safety implications of piloting living-room furniture down a public street.

“While we appreciate the creativity, we do have to remind everyone: no matter what you’re ‘driving’ (using that term extremely loosely), please think about safety first,” the department wrote.

If I was a college kid who bought a couch and needed to move it from one apt to another, this is an excellent alternative to carrying it.

Police also joked that it was “probably best to leave the mobile furniture displays to IKEA.”

It isn’t clear whether either rider was cited or arrested, and police didn’t publicly identify the pair.

But while a two-scooter electric couch probably isn’t the micromobility revolution cities had in mind, you’ve got to admit: door-to-door transportation doesn’t get much more comfortable than this.

#5207. Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood Studios Quietly Ban Staff From Betting on Prediction Markets: Report

Hollywood studios and production companies have been quietly issuing guidelines barring employees from betting on prediction markets, entertainment industry trade paper The Hollywood Reporter reported on Thursday.

The warnings prohibit staff from betting on events such as studio earnings, casting decisions and release dates, or passing information to friends and family so they can bet instead, according to the report. At least one company has reportedly set up a hotline for employees to anonymously report colleagues suspected of placing bets.

The Hollywood Reporter's own parent company, Penske Media, has also updated its employee handbook in recent weeks to ban bets on "award nominations, casting decisions, production timelines and release dates," the outlet said.

The rules extend long-standing bans on insider trading in the stock market to platforms such as Kalshi and Polymarket, which let users bet on everything from who will be cast as the next James Bond to who will be voted off a reality show.

"You now have, essentially, an explosion in the number of employers who have to think about insider trading rules from an ethical standpoint," Joshua Mitts, a law professor at Columbia University who studies prediction markets, told the outlet.

Enforcing those rules may be another matter. "With blockchain-based trading, it can be basically impossible if you know how to use a mixer correctly and know how to hide your identity behind multiple layers of intermediation," Mitts said.

Some bookmakers are already spooked. Betting site BetOnline temporarily pulled its market on the next James Bond this week after a sudden wave of "sharp money" pushed actor Callum Turner above longtime favorite Jack Lowden, raising concerns about a leak, according to the report.

Entertainment insiders have been caught before. In February, Kalshi said a video editor at MrBeast's company had made about $5,000 using confidential information to bet on MrBeast content; he was fired and fined $20,000.

Federal prosecutors have also brought cases over insider trading on prediction markets. In April, an active-duty U.S. soldier was charged with using classified information about the raid to capture Nicolás Maduro to bet on Polymarket. In May, a Google engineer was charged with commodities fraud, wire fraud and money laundering over Polymarket bets allegedly placed using confidential company data. Both cases are pending.

Governments have moved too. California, New York and Illinois have banned state employees from using nonpublic information to bet on prediction markets, while the platforms have rolled out their own surveillance tools. Polymarket has investigated dozens of bets on the U.S. midterm elections.

One kind of entertainment bet is already off the table. Under the Onion Futures Act of 1958, U.S. law explicitly bans just two types of futures contracts: those on onions and those on movie box office receipts, The Hollywood Reporter noted.

#5204. Bitcoin erases post-election gains as Wall Street selloff triggers over $1 billion in crypto liquidations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The selloff triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets, as total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached $1.09 billion, affecting 211,000 traders.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, and other cryptocurrencies erased their post-election gains on Thursday as a sharp selloff in U.S. technology and AI stocks spilled into cryptocurrency markets, triggering more than $1 billion in leveraged position liquidations over the past 24 hours.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell 6% to $75,700, after touching an intraday low of $75,568, while ether (ETH) dropped more than 7% to $2,247. The declines reversed Wednesday's rally, when bitcoin climbed back above $80,000 following the U.S. midterm elections and signs that the Binance hack had not caused broader market disruption.

Midterm election results appeared to generate short-term optimism, but analysts say they haven't resolved macroeconomic uncertainties.

As of 2 p.m. ET, the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 were down about 3% for the day, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite had dropped 4.7%. The Cboe Volatility Index, or VIX, surged approximately 60%, reflecting heightened investor anxiety.

AI and bitcoin-related mining stocks were among the hardest hit, with Bitdeer and Keel Infrastructure each falling about 20%. American Bitcoin dropped 17% despite announcing a quarterly profit of $126 million. Crypto bellwethers Strategy and Coinbase were each down about 10%.

Of note, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin hit an all-time low after falling more than 20% over the past two days.

The selloff also triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets. About $879.92 million in positions were liquidated over a four-hour period, including $818.90 million in longs and $61.02 million in shorts, according to CoinGlass. Total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached nearly $1.1 billion, affecting nearly 211,000 traders.

It should be noted that liquidation figures are typically based on publicly available data, which may significantly understate the extent of market liquidations.

The reversal suggests Wednesday's rebound may have been driven more by temporary optimism than a meaningful improvement in the market's underlying conditions, according to Wintermute.

The trading firm said the rally reflected a combination of relief following the Binance hack, enthusiasm surrounding the election results, optimism about a potential slowdown in AI spending and falling oil prices. However, those factors proved short-lived as broader macroeconomic pressures persisted.

"It's probably a combination of all of them in some way. What matters is that all of them seemed to be so short-lived that it actually didn't really matter," said Jasper De Maere, an OTC trader at Wintermute. "Trump is still the president for two years, the Middle East is still escalating, inflation is elevated into a strong U.S. economy."

Beyond the immediate market pressures, the election results may also complicate the outlook for crypto regulation, potentially tempering some of the optimism that helped drive Wednesday's rebound.

Paul Howard, senior director at Wincent, said the Democrats' strengthened position in Congress could make bipartisan cooperation necessary for meaningful progress on crypto legislation over the next two years. With the Clarity Act's prospects uncertain, Howard expects the existing regulatory framework to largely remain intact until the next election cycle.

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, said the election could delay crypto legislation but wouldn't fundamentally change longer-term institutional demand for digital assets.

"Investors are still focused on liquidity, interest rates and the broader economy, and those factors will continue to influence the market regardless of which party controls Congress," Kirkley said.

If the weakness continues, Wintermute identified $73,000 and $68,000 as the next levels to watch for bitcoin. The firm said it would need to see bitcoin convincingly reclaim and hold $80,000 before turning more positive.

#5203. E-quipment highlight: the highest-rated mowers on Amazon are ELECTRIC (2026)

Despite decades of market dominance, it seems like the days of loud, finicky, gas-powered mowers may finally be behind us; and I say that because – for the second year in a row! – the highest-rated lawnmowers on Amazon won’t burn a drop of gas.

The latest round of dumb oil wars are ratcheting up fuel prices everywhere while, at the same time, state and local governments continue to tightening restrictions on noise and small-engine emissions. Combined, those things probably mean that your next lawn mower is more likely to plug in than fill up, whether you like it or not. The good news is that. in addition to being quieter, safer, and not poisoning the air you breathe while you walk behind them, electric laenmowers have come a long way in terms of power and performance in just a few years. So much so, in fact, that many of the highest-rated models on Amazon, emphatic period, are electric.

In putting this list together, we looked at the average star ratings of a number of lawn mowers on Amazon, focusing on products that had at least 2,000 reviews and a four-star average or higher, and found that (like last year) battery-electric units from EGO Power, Greenworks, and Worx were among the top-rated mowers, regardless of fuel type.

Here’s the rundown, in alphabetical order (by brand).

Over the past few years, the EGO POWER+ brand has staked a claim to being the gold standard for cordless lawn equipment, thanks to its high-voltage 56V battery platform and premium build quality. The brand’s 21″ POWER+ LM2112 push mower currently carries a 4.5-star average rating from more than 2,200 Amazon reviews and an “Overall Pick” designation.

That’s no surprise, given the LM2112’s 6 ft-lbs. of cutting torque (equivalent to a 150cc ICE), and up to 35 minutes of runtime (call it a suburban yard) from the included 56V 4.0 Ah li-ion battery, three-in-one mulching, bagging and side-discharge capability. It also starts at the push of a button, folds for compact storage and skips the exhaust, maintenance, and oil changes that come along with a comparable gas mower.

With an impressive 4.2-star rating after more than 12,000 verified reviews, the Greenworks 40V 16″ push mower is in rare company among mowers of any kind on Amazon. No surprise, given that the lightweight, cordless mower weighs just 37.5 lbs. and comes with a 4.0Ah battery and charger good for up to 40 minutes of runtime, making it ideal for homeowners with smaller yards who want to ditch gas without wrestling with a heavier machine.

The Greenpower’s 16″ cutting deck is designed for (if not limited to) compact lawns, with a five-position height adjustment and a two-in-one system offering you a choice of mulching (yes) or bagging (no) your lawn clippings.

For bigger, more demanding lawns, Greenworks offers a 4.4-star push mower with a 21″ steel cutting deck that’s powered by an 80V brushless motor and ships with a pair of 2.0 Ah batteries good for up to 40 minutes of runtime with both batteries. Three-in-one functionality for mulching, bagging, or side discharge is included, too, along with push-button starting, seven-position height adjustment, and a rapid charger that can get it back to work in about 30 minutes.

WORX’s 40V 17″ Power Share mower is another strong example of how electric lawn care can be both practical and affordable. The WG743 mates a pair of 20V, 4.0 Ah batteries to deliver 40V of power, and those same batteries work across the brand’s broader Power Share lineup of tools – a lineup that includes string trimmers, power drills, and more.

The WORX mower’s standout feature, though, is its “IntelliCut” technology that works a bit like traction control in your car, automatically increasing torque when the mower hits thicker grass (more traction) and backing off when it doesn’t need the extra power (when it detects wheelspin – or, in this case, cutting head spin). Factor in an intentionally lightweight design, seven-position cutting-height adjustment, and the de rigueur options for mulching or bagging, and you’ve got a versatile little mower for keeping a smaller lawn in shape.

#5201. South Korean Crypto Exchange Gopax Suspends Withdrawals and Trading After Hacking Threat

South Korea's Gopax halted standard withdrawals at 2:55 p.m. and trading at 3:53 p.m. Friday after a hacking-threat email, and is transferring all held assets to cold wallets with no loss reported so far.

South Korean cryptocurrency exchange Gopax suspended withdrawals and trading on Friday after receiving a hacking-threat email, and said it is moving all held virtual assets into offline cold wallets as a preemptive step to protect customer funds.

Gopax halted standard deposit and withdrawal services at 2:55 p.m. local time and stopped trading at 3:53 p.m., Yonhap News Agency reported, citing the exchange. Direct withdrawals through the website and app are unavailable. Customers who want coins out must apply through the customer support center, where requests are processed manually after internal verification.

“To ensure the safe custody of user assets, we plan to transfer all virtual assets to cold wallets for storage,” Gopax said in remarks carried by Seoul Economic Daily. “Direct withdrawals through the Gopax website or app are temporarily unavailable, and customers wishing to withdraw funds should apply through the Gopax customer support center.”

No actual loss of assets has been reported. The company has not set a date to reopen trading or standard withdrawals.

With trading offline, all open orders were canceled, Chosun Biz reported. Deposits were initially paused with withdrawals; later Korean coverage said deposits had been restored while web and app withdrawals stayed shut. Manual withdrawals through support will take longer than usual.

A cold wallet is a storage setup kept offline, cut off from the internet that hot wallets use to process customer sends. Gopax said the shift of remaining hot-wallet balances into cold storage is meant to shrink the surface available to anyone who can reach online systems.

A Gopax official told Yonhap the email came from a party claiming it could hack the exchange, but that the threat itself had not been verified. “We ask for your understanding, as this measure is intended to prevent financial incidents and protect user assets,” the company said.

The freeze lands in a week when exchange security is already under a bright light. On Oct. 29, attackers drained about $6.34 billion from Binance hot wallets in what Binance later described as a months-long campaign that began with a fake job interview. The FBI has tied that theft to North Korea; Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it is covering customer losses from its own funds.

Brazil’s Mercado Bitcoin froze deposits and withdrawals earlier this week after its own security incident, with preliminary estimates of suspicious transfers near $40 million. Gopax’s case is different on the facts disclosed so far: the exchange is describing a threat email and a precautionary lockdown, not a confirmed drain.

Gopax itself has been dealing with account trouble. From early September, abnormal access hit some user accounts, leaking personal information on seven customers and two current or former employees, with further leakage confirmed last month, Yonhap’s Korean dispatch said. The company has treated that breach as separate from Friday’s threat email; any link has not been confirmed.

South Korea’s Financial Services Commission has also been pressing domestic platforms after a broader wave of cybersecurity incidents across financial firms. Korean exchanges are already required to keep at least 80% of customer crypto in cold storage. Friday’s move pushes Gopax further offline while it checks whether the threat is real.

For bitcoin holders on Gopax, the practical effect is a queue: coins are being parked offline while support staff approve outbound sends one by one. That is slower than an app button, and it is the trade the exchange says it is making to keep balances intact.

The test for Gopax now is whether it can finish the cold-wallet transfer, clear the manual withdrawal queue without a loss, and say when trading comes back — or whether the unverified threat turns into the incident it was trying to get ahead of.
