#5161. Thailand SEC Files Criminal Complaint Against Bitget Ahead of Dec. 5 Access Block

Thailand's SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget operator BG Limited for unlicensed Thai trading from 2022 to 2025.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission filed a criminal complaint Thursday against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, alleging it ran an unlicensed digital-asset exchange for Thai users from 2022 to 2025, and notified the government to block the platform from Dec. 5.

The complaint, lodged with the Economic Crime Suppression Division, also names Mr. Kritsana Kritsananuwat as an alleged supporter who promoted Bitget through Facebook and YouTube channels, the SEC said in statement No. 241/2026.

From 2022 through 2025, the regulator found, Bitget offered a trading system in the ordinary course of business, charged fees, ran a Thai-language website that could show values in baht, and solicited Thai users on social channels including “Bitget Thailand” on X, “BGB Thai Holder” on Telegram and “bitgetthailand” on TikTok.

That conduct amounts to operating a digital-asset exchange under Section 3 of the Emergency Decree on Digital Asset Businesses B.E. 2561 (2018) without a license, a violation of Section 26 punishable under Section 66, the SEC said. The case now moves to an inquiry official, then prosecutors and the courts.

Separately, the SEC submitted Bitget’s website, other domains and its app to the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society under a 2025 royal decree that authorizes access blocks on unlicensed trading platforms. From Dec. 5, the public “will be unable to access the platform,” the statement said, and investors still holding assets there were told to consider acting before the cutoff.

The enforcement push lands weeks after Bitget announced a planned acquisition of GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics (Thailand), a Bangkok digital-asset exchange and brokerage that already holds Thai licenses, through its interest in BTGT Management (Thailand) Ltd.

Under that deal, GMO Internet and its Thai holding company would transfer their entire stake in GMO-Z — about 49% and 50.94%, respectively — to BTGT or a designee at completion, a Sept. 24 GMO Internet filing showed. Bitget said completion remains subject to regulatory approval and, after a change-of-control nod, it intends to introduce its trading platform to Thai users under local rules.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said in the Oct. 20 release.

The timetable has already slipped. GMO Internet Group pushed the effective date of the share transfer from Oct. 31 to Nov. 30, citing careful work on Thai regulatory applications and closing procedures. That revised date sits five days before the planned Bitget access block.

CoinDesk reported that Bitget’s own terms of use, updated in October, already list Thailand among jurisdictions where its services are prohibited, and that the SEC has not said whether the criminal complaint will weigh on the GMO-Z change of control.

Thailand has used the same access-blocking tool before. In mid-2025 the SEC referred Bybit, OKX and three other unlicensed platforms for a June 28 cutoff, telling users to move assets first. The Bitget action follows that template — and adds a criminal complaint against the exchange operator and a named promoter.

Licensed venues still sit at the center of Bangkok’s policy mix. Thailand has approved a multi-year capital-gains tax break only for trades on SEC-regulated platforms, and has been expanding regulated crypto products in derivatives and capital markets. Unlicensed platforms get the opposite treatment: investor alerts, access blocks and, now for Bitget, a police referral.

What is not yet clear is whether a criminal case against BG Limited for the 2022–2025 period bars, slows or leaves untouched BTGT’s bid to take over a licensed local operator. The SEC statement does not mention GMO-Z. Bitget has not publicly answered the complaint in a statement posted with the acquisition release.

For Thai users still on Bitget, the practical clock is Dec. 5. For Bitget, the test is whether Nov. 30 closes a regulated door into Thailand — or whether the complaint filed Thursday makes that licensed path the next question the ECD and the SEC have to answer.

#5160. DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

DOJ Creates 'Super Intelligence' Task Force to Guard Against AI Abuse

The U.S. Department of Justice is setting up a task force dedicated to policing the misuse of advanced artificial intelligence, as Washington races to build enforcement muscle around a technology it increasingly frames in near-sci-fi terms.

Announced Thursday, the Super Intelligence Task Force, or DOJ-SITF, will be led by Associate Attorney General Stanley Woodward and Associate Deputy Attorney General Stephen Weiss.

Its stated mission is to make sure American leadership in “super intelligence” isn’t compromised by bad actors, whether foreign governments and their proxies, transnational criminal organizations, or companies and individuals using AI to commit fraud or other crimes.

The department invoked President Donald Trump’s framing that “America stands at the forefront of a new technological revolution in intelligence.”

“SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators,” Attorney General Todd Blanche said in a statement, adding that the task force would work alongside SI Force Chair Jay Clayton to keep critical AI technologies from being “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

Blanche said the DOJ would keep “identifying and prosecuting individuals whose use of SI violates the law, including by maliciously targeting American taxpayers, critical U.S. infrastructure, or other aspects of our national security.”

Notably, the effort isn’t purely about catching criminals. Weiss said the department also has “a responsibility” to “harden regular reporting on SI risk and response, and ensure companies exercise vigorous internal controls so that SI technology behaves as intended,” signaling that the DOJ wants AI developers themselves to tighten safeguards against models going off the rails.

The term “super intelligence” typically refers to a hypothetical AI that surpasses human capability across the board, and the government’s embrace of the phrase marks an escalation in how officials talk about the technology. Former SEC chair Jay Clayton has been tied to a broader Trump administration push around super intelligence, and the SEC recently added “super intelligence” to its 2027 examination priorities.

The task force joins a widening federal scramble to get ahead of AI risk. Trump signed an executive order setting up a fight with states over AI regulation. Earlier this week, the U.S. banking regulator OCC issued its first AI framework as banks battle rogue agents reaching into customer accounts.

The DOJ did not specify a timeline or detail how the task force would coordinate with other agencies, saying only that its work would “promote innovation and prevent the misuse” of the technology.

#5152. OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI Promises to Foot AI’s Power Bill After Voter Revolt. Who Enforces It?

OpenAI promised Thursday that neighbors of its future AI campuses won't foot the bill for their electricity needs, two days after voters dealt data center expansion a blow at the polls. It also pledged public energy and water figures and limits on secrecy agreements with officials.

Its National Community Compact covers future campuses (a fancy rebranding for the term AI data centers) OpenAI both owns and operates, according to the company’s announcement. A separate policy blueprint asks legislators and regulators to apply similar requirements across the industry.

“We will pay the full cost of powering our compute campuses and ensure local residents do not foot the bill for our energy needs,” OpenAI said in its “national community compact.”

It also promises financial assurances before costly grid upgrades, so other customers aren't left paying if projects shrink or fail. Each AI data center would get a water plan, annual progress reports and independent noise studies.

The national compact does not describe penalties for noncompliance, a dispute process or independent verification of electricity and water figures. Residents could read OpenAI’s disclosures, but the document does not explain how they could compel corrections or recover losses.

The ownership restriction matters, too. Partners including Oracle build and own much of the infrastructure reserved for OpenAI, Reuters reported. The company has not said its national compact automatically covers those partner-owned facilities.

The proposed blueprint goes further on electricity costs. “Policymakers should direct state public utility commissions to create large-load tariffs that require large electricity consumers to pay the incremental costs they create,” it says.

A large-load tariff is a special electricity rate for major users such as data centers. The blueprint also urges mandatory reporting, narrow limits on officials’ NDAs and provisions letting governments reclaim tax breaks when developers miss agreed commitments.

The announcement follows a bruising election for the industry. Voters backed restrictions on data centers in multiple states, while candidates pushing for tighter oversight won key races. The results basically send a message to OpenAI and the broader AI industry that communities want a bigger say in how these facilities are built and how their operations affect the people living nearby.

OpenAI says every future campus it owns and operates will receive its own local compact and an annual public progress report.

#5150. BYD opens pre-orders for its first luxury electric SUV with over 1,000 km CLTC range

BYD opened pre-orders for the new Denza N8, a luxury five-seat electric SUV with a CLTC range of over 1,000 km (621 miles).

“The king of large 5-seater pure electric vehicles,” as BYD’s luxury sub-brand Denza calls it, is now available to pre-order in China.

BYD opened pre-orders for the new Denza N8 on Monday, and the results so far “have been excellent,” according to Li Hui, Denza’s general manager.

The new Denza N8 is “definitely the most anticipated luxury SUV of the year,” Li said. Denza confirmed in August that the new version will use its Blade Battery 2.0 and Flash Charging Technology to deliver “a world-leading 1,003 km pure electric range.”

For context, the plug-in hybrid version the new Denza N8 is replacing offered a combined CLTC range of 1,003 km (623 miles) on a full tank of gas and a full charge.

BYD didn’t reveal pre-order prices, but Li confirmed last week that the Denza N8’s official launch is scheduled for November.

It will go on sale after the new Denza N8L, a slightly longer six-seat version. The N8L is available in two versions: Premium and Flagship. The N8L EV Premium starts at 299,800 RMB, or about $45,000, while the Flagship model is priced from 329,800 RMB ($49,000).

Those who place a reservation with a 2,000 RMB ($200) deposit will receive a 5,000 RMB credit as a limited-time special offer.

Other premium features include zero gravity seating in all rows, a three-spoke steering wheel, wireless phone chargers, and ambient lighting.

The luxury SUV is equipped with a roof-mounted LiDAR to support BYD’s God’s Eye 5.0 smart driving assistance system. It also features DiSus-A, BYD’s intelligent air suspension system, and rear-wheel drive.

The new Denza N8 EV will be available with a single rear-wheel-drive (RWD) or a tri-motor all-wheel-drive (AWD) powertrain. The RWD version uses a single motor producing either 320 kW (429 hp) or 370 kW (496 hp).

The tri-motor Denza N8 uses BYD’s e3 platform. The setup includes a 270 kW (362 hp) front motor and two 310 kW (416 hp) rear motors, delivering a combined output of 890 kW (1,193 hp).

Regulatory filings earlier this year revealed it will be available with a 130.15 kWh or 105.79 kWh BYD Blade 2.0 battery, offering CLTC ranges of up to 1,003 km and 800 km, respectively.

BYD will announce prices next month, but given that the Denza N8L starts at 299,800 RMB ($45,000), the standard version will likely be priced below it.

#5147. U.S. Treasury Cuts Off MEXC From U.S. Financial System Over Alleged $16 Billion in Illicit Flows

The U.S. Treasury’s Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued a final order Friday that will cut offshore exchange MEXC Global off from the American banking system over alleged links to sanctions evasion by Iran, North Korea and Russia, the department said.

FinCEN’s order, published as a PDF pending Federal Register placement, finds MEXC a “primary money laundering concern in connection with Russian illicit finance” under section 9714(a) of the Combating Russian Money Laundering Act. Thirty days after that Register notice, covered U.S. financial institutions will be barred from any funds transfer — including convertible virtual currency — to or from MEXC.

Reuters reported the same cut-off Friday afternoon. Treasury framed the move as part of Operation Economic Outcast. It is not an OFAC sanctions designation: the order says it does not rewrite Bank Secrecy Act duties or OFAC rules, and it is a FinCEN funds-transmittal ban rather than a Section 311 proposed rulemaking.

Treasury alleges MEXC facilitated at least $16 billion in illicit flows from May 2021 through May 2026, including nearly $1.5 billion tied to Russian sanctions evasion and about $22 million linked to Iran’s Central Bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps’ Qods Force. The platform was still processing trades with sanctioned Garantex and successor Grinex as recently as June 2026, more than four years after OFAC designated Garantex, the release says.

The order, signed by FinCEN Deputy Director Jimmy L. Kirby, covers MEXC Global and named affiliates operating outside the United States, including Seychelles-incorporated MEXC Global Ltd / MX Global Ltd, Estonia’s MEXC Estonia OÜ, Switzerland’s GIDA Technology AG and the U.K.’s MXC Global Ltd, plus successors. Covered institutions face civil or criminal penalties for willful violations.

Until the Federal Register publishes the notice, the 30-day clock has not started. As of Friday evening eastern time the Register’s public index still showed no MEXC document, matching the order’s own cover note that publication is pending.

Founded in 2018 and formerly branded MXC, MEXC ranks among the world’s 20 largest centralized crypto venues by volume. Treasury puts daily turnover above $1 billion and reserves near $5 billion as of mid-2026, with access claimed across more than 170 countries. The exchange already lists the United States as a prohibited jurisdiction in its user terms, so Friday’s action mainly hits any remaining dollar rails, banking partners and counterparties that still touch U.S. institutions.

Regulators elsewhere have been circling for years. The U.K. Financial Conduct Authority put MEXC Global Ltd on its warning list in March 2024 as unauthorized. Hong Kong’s Securities and Futures Commission warned the same month that the platform was marketing to local investors without a licence. Seychelles’ Financial Services Authority said in May 2026 it took action against MX Global Ltd for unlicensed virtual-asset operations — footnoted in FinCEN’s order.

Those compliance gaps matter because the illicit-flow thesis is specific, not guilt by association. Beyond Russia, FinCEN cites about $1 billion in direct traffic with more than 40 Iranian crypto exchangers, nearly $26 million with the OFAC-sanctioned A7 sanctions-evasion network, and almost $200 million tied to the A7A5 stablecoin. DPRK facilitators and cybercrime flows are also alleged. MEXC has not issued a public response to the Friday order; its main X account kept posting marketing content through the afternoon.

Practically, once the order bites, U.S. banks, money transmitters and other covered firms cannot move dollars or crypto to MEXC wallets or accounts. Offshore users may keep trading peer-to-peer, but dollar on-ramps and any bank that still clears for MEXC become legal risk. The template tracks earlier 9714 cut-offs of venues such as Bitzlato, and it lands beside a busy sanctions week that has already seen North Korea crypto designations, an Iranian exchange action, and Russia’s push to put bitcoin trading on the Moscow Exchange.

Whether MEXC contests the finding, seeks an exemption, or quietly reroutes liquidity is the open question. For now the paper trail is clear: a final FinCEN order under 9714(a), a 30-day fuse after Federal Register publication, and Treasury’s claim of at least $16 billion in illicit volume sitting behind the cut-off.

#5145. Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Bitcoin Fixes This? Paraguay President Resigns After Bank Lockout Hits 60% of Country

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday after the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank left roughly three million customers unable to access their accounts, turning a banking failure into a political emergency.

The announcement did not end the confrontation. Paraguay’s lower house voted 78-0 to impeach Peña minutes later, sending the charges to the Senate as Vice President Pedro Alliana prepared to take over, per AP.

The bank’s shutdown has also revived an argument familiar to Bitcoin holders: Money held by a financial intermediary is only as accessible as that institution allows. Self-custodied Bitcoin works differently, although it comes with risks of its own.

As the Bitcoin OGs would say, “Bitcoin fixes this.”

Paraguay’s central bank placed Ueno into resolution after regulators found its solvency ratio had fallen below half the legal minimum. The process, announced on November 3, followed accounting adjustments to the bank’s financial statements and left its app, cards, and transfers out of service.

Ueno claimed around 2.9 million customers, more than 60% of Paraguay’s adults. It also held roughly $800 million in public-sector deposits, including pension and health funds, according to AP, complicating an already painful freeze on ordinary savings and wages.

Peña previously held a stake in a company linked to Ueno’s owners, a relationship that fueled allegations of conflicts of interest. He has denied wrongdoing and said he sold his interest in March 2025, according to Reuters.

“My greatest wish is that the return of those funds be guaranteed, that the Deposit Guarantee Fund fulfill its purpose,” Peña said in his resignation address. The Deposit Guarantee Fund would be the equivalent of the FDIC in the United States.

Mensaje del Presidente de la República, Santiago Peña, al pueblo paraguayo. pic.twitter.com/RBz6IfFV9L

— Santiago Peña (@SantiPenap) November 5, 2026

Outside Congress, the anger was less diplomatic. “Santiago Peña and the Colorado Party have stolen all our savings,” protester Luis Pineda told AP. “We are fed up with 70 years of plunder.”

The central bank says Ueno’s failure is an isolated case, not evidence of a broader banking collapse. It has announced a deposit guarantee of up to 228.3 million guaraníes per depositor, roughly $36,000, with initial payments targeting eligible accounts holding no more than 10 million guaraníes.

Bitcoin was born during another banking crisis. Its first block, mined in January 2009, included a newspaper headline about a second bailout for British banks, a historical detail long cited by supporters who distrust reliance on financial intermediaries.

A Bitcoin holder who controls a wallet’s private keys can authorize transfers from the wallet without asking a third party, like a bank, to unlock an account. That is the principle behind the crypto saying “not your keys, not your coins,” and the distinction from leaving Bitcoin on an exchange that can itself freeze withdrawals.

But Bitcoin is not perfect. Self-custody has its own risks: Forget your keys or lose them and there is no way you can recover your funds, there is nobody who can assist you, and no law enforcement agency who can do anything for you.

Paraguay’s central bank said Thursday its first guarantee payments would go to depositors with up to 10 million guaraníes, no Ueno debts and an account at another financial institution.

#5144. Tesla turns to car rental platform Turo for Full Self-Driving test drives

Turo will soon let US renters search specifically for Teslas with “Full Self-Driving” (Supervised) turned on, using a direct integration with Tesla to verify each car’s FSD status. The feature goes live on October 28.

For Tesla, it’s a new way to put FSD in front of people who don’t own a Tesla, and a new source of the FSD subscriptions that now count toward Elon Musk’s pay package.

Hosts link their Tesla account to Turo, and the platform pulls the car’s FSD status through Tesla’s API. Eligible listings then show that FSD is active, and guests can filter for it.

Turo says “thousands” of FSD-enabled Teslas are already listed. Not a shocker. The Model 3 and Model Y are the third and fourth most popular vehicles on Turo so far this year, according to the company.

Eligible US hosts can also get a free one-month FSD (Supervised) trial. After that, it’s Tesla’s regular $99 per month subscription, a cost hosts will want to recover through their daily rates. Turo didn’t say whether FSD listings will carry a premium.

Turo CEO Andre Haddad called it an “industry-first integration with Tesla” and said that “most travelers have had limited opportunity to experience driver’s assistance technology, but the demand is clear.”

The “industry-first” part is generous. Tesla has been renting out its own cars with FSD included in Southern California since last year, a program we first spotted in the works back in 2023. The new part is verified FSD on a third-party marketplace with far more cars in far more cities.

Tesla went subscription-only with FSD in February, and since then it has been pushing hard to get people to try it, including a free one-year subscription on new Model 3 and Model Y orders.

There’s a reason for the urgency. Tesla reported 1.74 million “active FSD subscriptions” in Q3, a metric it only started disclosing because Musk’s 2025 CEO Performance Award uses it. One of the award’s milestones is 10 million. At last quarter’s pace, Tesla would need about eight more years to get there.

Turo’s release leans heavily on “self-driving” and “driverless” language. It cites its own research claiming that 55% of Americans are interested in “driverless technology” for travel, rising to 71% among Gen Z, and that over a fifth of interested Americans would be comfortable spending seven or more hours in a self-driving car.

But FSD (Supervised) is not driverless. It’s a level 2 driver assistance system, and the person behind the wheel is responsible at all times. Even Haddad calls it “driver’s assistance technology” in the same release.

Renters will be getting into a car they’ve likely never driven, with a system they’ve likely never used. Turo’s safeguard is to encourage guests to review Tesla’s FSD documentation and safety guidelines before pickup. That’s it.

We saw what over-trust looks like just two weeks ago, when a driver told the CHP he was asleep with FSD engaged before his Tesla hit a patrol car inside a lane closure in Fairfield, California. Two officers were seriously injured.

Turo also didn’t say who’s on the hook when a guest crashes with FSD engaged: the renter, the host, or Turo’s protection plans.

This is basically ‘Tesla network’ but supervised. Since 2016, Elon Musk has been saying that Tesla owners are going to be able to put their vehicles in the ‘Tesla Network’ to provide rides for others and earn income.

This vision has been delayed for years, but the best they can do right now is combining Turo and FSD.

Usually when I rent a Tesla on Turo the owner has turned off all self steering features. So far, they appear to be more confident in my driving than letting FSD do the work. Given the risks of letting a person unfamiliar with your car drive it, I think if they sincerely believed FSD was safer they would have it on all the time and not let me near the steering wheel. This speaks volumes about FSD.

For Tesla, it should be good for FSD adoption. A weekend road trip with FSD is a much better test drive than 15 minutes around the block with a Tesla advisor, and Turo gets a feature no rental company has.

However, I don’t want to be a party pooper, but I do worry about the complacency issue. Turo is pitching “self-driving” to travelers, and Tesla still calls the product “Full Self-Driving” in the US, even after it dropped the name in Europe under pressure from German regulators. Put a tourist who has never used FSD behind the wheel with that framing, and some of them will treat it as a chauffeur. That’s exactly how you end up with a driver asleep at freeway speed.

At a minimum, Turo should require a short in-app FSD walkthrough before a guest can use it, and spell out liability before someone finds out the hard way. Tesla, which collects the subscriptions, should want the same.

If a weekend in a rented Tesla convinces you to go electric, powering your own EV with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5142. Elliptic says Binance hackers swapped stolen tokens for $124M in bitcoin, exchange plans more withdrawal reopenings

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The laundering operation spans 20,000 wallets and 16 blockchains, but Elliptic says little has likely been cashed out.

The attackers in last week's $6.43 billion breach of the Binance crypto exchange have managed to swap around $124 million in altcoins into bitcoin, according to an update from blockchain analysis firm Elliptic.

This is on top of the $1.48 billion in bitcoin stolen in the attack, which is being attributed to North Korea.

Elliptic added that the laundering operation has thus far used more than 20,000 wallets across 16 blockchains and 65,000 transactions, in an attempt to obfuscate the source of the funds. Despite this, the firm estimated that only a "negligible amount" has likely been cashed out.

The largest portion of the converted bitcoin came from stolen XRP swapped through THORChain. Elliptic's report said that by early Friday, roughly $50 million in XRP had been swapped, primarily into bitcoin, with about $226 million remaining.

The report added that nearly all of the stolen Solana (SOL) has already been mixed, swapped, or bridged into bitcoin.

Around $3.11 billion, mostly in ether and bitcoin, remains in addresses linked to the attack, while $2.85 billion of the total  $6.43 billion has been frozen. It's unclear if the $470 million not accounted for here is due to price changes since the attack, is from funds that have already been cashed out, or is a mix of both.

The Block has reached out to the report's author, Tom Robinson, for clarification.

Elliptic wrote that it expects the attackers to swap the rest of the ether for bitcoin in small batches before trying to cash it out through OTC brokers and Chinese money laundering networks.

The firm also rejected the speculation that AI agents were doing the laundering.

"The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," Elliptic said.

On Friday, Binance wrote on X that it was working to reopen withdrawals for HYPE, INJ, APT, PLASMA, NEAR and LTC by the "end of next week." It is also aiming to reopen withdrawals "for all tokens on networks that are already restored," including ETH, BSC, Arbitrum and Optimism.

The exchange's own report, published Tuesday, said the attack was carried out over months and involved a tampered developer tool, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that got the attackers access to Binance's wallet-signing infrastructure.

Binance has since replenished virtually all the assets stolen in the breach.

#5141. Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Claude Gets Personal Finance—Anthony Pompliano Accuses Anthropic of Copying His Startup

Anthropic launched a personal finance feature for Claude on Thursday, letting users connect bank accounts and ask the chatbot what they have spent, what bills are due, and how much money they have left.

The announcement came with an accusation from crypto investor Anthony Pompliano, who says Anthropic tried to get inside information from his competing startup, Silvia, months before the rollout.

Claude can then summarize spending, spot duplicate charges or unexpected fees, explain interest payments, and build charts or bill calendars. Anthropic's example shows a user asking where their money went this month, then following up to find out what remains before payday.

"Access is read-only, and Claude can't move, manage, or invest your money," Anthropic wrote on X. "Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations," the company added.

The assistant can see balances, credit limits, account names and types, the last four digits of account numbers, and up to 24 months of transactions from accounts users approve. According to Anthropic's documentation, conversations inside the Personal Finance hub are not used to train its models, but account balances may lag and AI-generated figures should be checked against bank records.

Also, how Anthropic tracks its users’ financial data and uses it beyond model training is not completely clear—and this is extremely valuable information for big data companies.

Pompliano, whose Silvia product bills itself as a personal AI chief financial officer, posted a lengthy complaint on X shortly after Claude's announcement. He alleged that Anthropic personnel began approaching Silvia engineers in April, supposedly because they were interested in the startup's growth and computing needs.

"Anthropic was fishing for details on how we built our product and why it was so popular," he wrote. Pompliano also described an alleged steak dinner involving Anthropic representatives, claiming someone told the Silvia team that the meal was on CEO Dario Amodei's tab.

His engineers declined to divulge sensitive information, he claimed, adding that someone at Anthropic warned his team about the upcoming launch days earlier.

"Today, Anthropic launched a Claude wrapper that is masquerading as a personal finance product," Pompliano wrote. He also claimed Silvia's computing costs are roughly 97% lower when using its own models and hardware instead of frontier AI models, which most likely mean they are using a local model, likely finetuned for the startup’s specific purpose.

That said, Anthropic was not the first general-purpose chatbot maker to move into personal finance, either. OpenAI launched similar bank connections for ChatGPT in May, also using Plaid for read-only access to financial information, and Agents like Hermes, Open Claw, or Grok Bot can also do pretty similar tasks given the proper skills, permissions, and instructions.

Anthropic says Personal Finance is a first step and is exploring partnerships with licensed professionals and regulated financial firms. For now, the beta is restricted to U.S. customers on three paid Claude plans.

#5138. Cybertruck driver stopped in Germany, ‘Monster truck’ not allowed on roads

A Tesla Cybertruck was pulled over while driving in Germany, a reminder that the vehicle is generally not legal on European roads.

The Tesla Cybertruck has attracted plenty of attention, mostly for its unconventional and polarizing look. For better or worse*, the car looks different than anything else on the road, and thus has been the target of all sorts of discussion (*it’s for worse).

But it’s not just the look of the car that’s unconventional. Because of that look, it also breaks several European rules, particularly those related to pedestrian protection (which was called out immediately upon reveal by many observers, including this author).

As a result, it does not have “type approval” in Germany. Even applications for individual exemptions have been denied because of the vehicle “deviates significantly” from requirements.

The vehicle is also too large. In general, a “class B” license, the normal license you get for driving a car, allows you to drive a vehicle up to 3,500kg (7,716lb). The Cybertruck does weigh less than this, but this number refers to the “Gross Vehicle Weight,” or the maximum weight of the truck when laden with occupants and cargo, for which all trims of the Cybertruck exceed 3,500kg.

A Swedish driver ran up against this barrier when driving through Bavaria, near the town of Vaterstatten, Merkur.de reports (with the funny title “Tesla monster truck stopped on A99”). The driver was holding a class B license, like most drivers, whereas the police said that a C1 license allowing operation of a vehicle up to 7.5 tons would be required.

The police did not allow the driver to continue on their journey. Vaterstatten is near the Southern border of Germany, so the Cybertruck might not have been on German roads for long… except the driver is Swedish, so if they drove all the way from Sweden, they would have spent quite a while on German roads before being stopped.

German here: Technically it is possible to register a modified Cybertruck in a European country and drive it legally on European roads. But you can't register it in Germany, you are only allowed to drive it here occasionally and for less than one year. And you still need a truck driver license (typ "C1" for up to 7.5 tonnes) or an older German license "Führerscheinklasse 3", and it has to be limited to max 90 km/h (approx 56 mph).

This guy from Sweden has somehow managed to register it in Poland. Still the car was not limited in top speed and he didn't have the necessary license for more than 3,5 t maximum weight. So he wasn't allowed to drive any further and probably had to pay high fines.

We’ve seen similar situations before in Europe, with an imported Cybertruck being seized in the UK, someone trying to smuggle two through Lithuania into Russia, and with the US Army warning servicemembers that the Cybertruck is not allowed in Germany.

Nevertheless a few people still manage to get Cybertrucks onto European roads, by getting individual approval from one country or another, then driving across borders. But even in cases like that, a story like this shows that drivers could open themselves up to trouble by driving this unapproved vehicle on European roads.

#5137. Trump administration moves closer to allowing crypto in 401(k) retirement plans

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets.

A proposed rule that would open up 401(k) retirement accounts to alternative assets, including cryptocurrencies, has landed at the White House for consideration.

On Thursday, the Department of Labor filed a proposal with the White House's Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs (OIRA), a division within the Office of Management and Budget that reviews federal regulations before publication, to further President Donald Trump's executive order directing the department to pave the way for including alternative assets in 401(k) plans.

"Such clarification must aim to identify the criteria that fiduciaries should use to prudently balance potentially higher expenses against the objectives of seeking greater long-term net returns and broader diversification of investments," the DOL said in its filing.

This comes months after the DOL unveiled its initial rule outlining steps those 401(k) plan managers should take when considering incorporating alternative assets such as digital assets and private equity into their investment profiles. Trump, earlier, had directed the DOL through his executive order.

The DOL also earlier reversed guidance discouraging retirement managers from considering crypto as an investment option in 401(k) plans after the department, under the Biden Administration, had raised concerns about crypto in those retirement plans.

The proposal to allow for crypto in 401(k) plans has garnered scrutiny on Capitol Hill. Earlier this year, Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pushed the DOL to scrap the rule and said it would expose investors to volatile assets, including crypto.

In the filing on Thursday, when describing the costs and benefits, the DOL said it was still considering the costs.

#5135. Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

Zcash's NU7 Upgrade Goes Live, Recycling 60% of Fees to Fund Future Block Rewards

"NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here," the project's official account tweeted.

NU7 has successfully activated on mainnet. A new era of Zcash is here. $ZEC pic.twitter.com/rPaSFe9h7j

"This is a huge milestone for Shielded Labs," Jason McGee of Shielded Labs, the Zcash development organization behind the mechanism, tweeted ahead of the activation.

The NSM tackles a problem Zcash inherited from Bitcoin: whether, as block subsidies decline with each halving, transaction fees alone will be enough to pay miners to secure the network. Under the mechanism, 60% of transaction fees will be removed from circulation and gradually reissued through future block rewards, while preserving the 21 million ZEC supply cap. Reissuance will begin in 2031, with the recycled funds spread along a smooth curve on top of the existing halving schedule.

The NSM was Shielded Labs' first project and builds on an idea that developer Nate Wilcox proposed years ago, originally called the Zcash Posterity Fund, McGee said. A separate proposal under consideration would require Zcash Community Grants to contribute 50,000 ZEC from its treasury to the NSM.

— Jason McGee 🛡 (@aquietinvestor) November 5, 2026

NU7 also limits how much private transaction activity can fit into each block, reducing the data wallets must scan to find their payments, according to the upgrade specification.

Shielded Labs' original design would have replaced Zcash's halvings with a smooth issuance curve. Community panels supported that change, but coinholders did not, so an alternative design that kept the halvings was developed instead.

"We still think the original design was simpler and better, but we respect the process that led us here," McGee wrote. "It should be difficult to change the protocol, and the process should resist capture by any single organization, group, or influential individual."

The upgrade arrives as Zcash's community debates the future of Zcash Community Grants (ZCG) and its share of the Dev Fund, the slice of block rewards that pays for development.

The five organizations that coordinate Zcash's coinholder voting, Project Tachyon, Shielded Labs, Valar Group, the Zcash Foundation and ZODL, have set out an expedited timeline for proposals and polling, saying "tensions were high" and that a drawn-out debate risked "deepening divisions within the Zcash community."

Proposals are due by November 8, with polls for coinholders and community panels running from November 16 to November 30. If rough consensus emerges, the groups will support a further network upgrade, NU 7.1, to implement it.

#5133. Paraguay president resigns after collapse of Ueno digital bank

Santiago Peña stepped down amid mounting scrutiny over his former ties to Ueno Bank’s parent company, as the lender’s collapse left millions of customers unable to access their funds.

Update (Nov. 5, 21:21 UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from X4T founder and CEO David Eisenmann and Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino.

Paraguayan President Santiago Peña announced his resignation Thursday amid a deepening political crisis triggered by the collapse of digital lender Ueno Bank.

In a video posted to X, Peña said he was stepping down to preserve the country’s stability, social peace and democratic institutions. He added that his priority was protecting Paraguayans who had entrusted their savings to the financial system.

Paraguay’s central bank announced resolution proceedings for Ueno Bank on Tuesday after finding that its solvency ratio had fallen below half the 12% regulatory minimum.

Peña faced mounting political scrutiny over his former business ties to Grupo Vázquez, the conglomerate that controls Ueno Bank. He remained a business partner of the group until March 2025, according to AP.

Peña has denied any wrongdoing, arguing that the controversy began as a regulatory matter before escalating into a political dispute.

The banking crisis also coincided with elevated asking prices for Tether’s USDt stablecoin on Bybit’s Paraguay peer-to-peer marketplace. One listing viewed early Thursday offered USDt at 6,942 Paraguayan guaraníes, approximately 13% above the central bank’s Nov. 4 reference rate of 6,144 guaraníes per US dollar.

His resignation came as lawmakers debated impeachment proceedings over alleged conflicts of interest, misuse of pension funds and institutional cover-ups linked to Ueno Bank’s collapse, according to Reuters.

Peña, who was elected in April 2023 and took office that August, was serving a five-year term scheduled to end in 2028. Under Paraguay’s Constitution, Vice President Pedro Alliana would succeed him for the remainder of the term if Congress accepts his resignation.

Ueno Bank had grown rapidly since 2021 into one of Paraguay’s largest financial institutions, serving more than 3 million customers in a country of roughly 6.5 million people.

Ueno first came under regulatory scrutiny in June over loans to companies linked to its controlling shareholder, Grupo Vázquez. In an October statement, the bank acknowledged receiving regulatory notices on June 5 and July 24 requiring adjustments to its related-party lending exposure.

On Nov. 2, the Central Bank of Paraguay initiated resolution proceedings after adjustments to the bank’s financial statements pushed its solvency ratio down to 5.56%, less than half of the 12% regulatory minimum. Authorities suspended the bank’s operations, restricting customers’ access to their deposits.

David Eisenmann, founder and CEO of Paraguayan crypto exchange X4T, told Cointelegraph that customers are turning to crypto to pay bills and protect their savings amid the banking crisis. Some are cashing out their holdings into guaraníes or US dollars to pay salaries and settle debts, while others are buying dollar-backed stablecoins.

“Customers are turning to their crypto holdings and converting them into guaraníes or dollars to make payments they couldn’t make with their bank deposits,” he said. “When that access is restricted, people look for other sources of liquidity.”

On X4T, USDT and USDC prices in guaraníes have risen about 8% in recent weeks, Eisenmann said, reflecting the dollar’s gains against the local currency. That recovery began in October and accelerated this week, so the increase could not be attributed entirely to Ueno Bank’s collapse, he added.

The dollar gained about 1.6% against the guaraní between Oct. 1 and the month’s final trading day, central bank data shows. Despite its rally this week, it remained about 2.8% below in 2026.

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also weighed in, writing on X that “USDt is a lifeline for hundreds millions of people in the world,” in response to Cointelegraph’s coverage of the banking turmoil.

On Thursday, Paraguay’s central bank announced that it would start deposit guarantee payments to eligible clients with balances of up to 10 million guaraníes (about $1,630), provided they had no outstanding debts to Ueno and held accounts at other financial institutions.

Under the country’s deposit insurance system, deposits are protected up to 228.3 million guaraníes (about $37,200) per depositor, per financial institution.

The bank’s collapse sparked protests in the nation’s capital, Asunción, where hundreds of depositors, pensioners and workers demanded the return of their savings and accountability from government officials.

Paraguayan authorities detained eight Ueno Bank executives on Nov. 3, including Grupo Vázquez president Miguel Vázquez and bank president Juan Manuel Gustale, according to local newspaper ABC Color. A court subsequently ordered six executives held in pretrial detention and placed two others under house arrest.

#5131. Tesla makes another joke of its Robotaxi geofence expansion

Tesla expanded its Robotaxi service area in Houston today, and it shaped the new geofence like a rocket. Plenty of people on X see something else in it, which is fair given the phallic Austin map Tesla posted in 2025.

It’s a cute nod to Houston’s space history. It’s also a service area drawn for a meme, and Robotaxi Tracker counts only 34 driverless Model Ys to cover it.

Tesla’s official Robotaxi account announced the expansion on X this morning with “Houston, we have liftoff” and a map comparing the old and new geofences:

Houston, we have liftoff Bigger service area now live pic.twitter.com/ps8fZmWpuP

When Tesla launched in Houston in April, the service covered a small zone of about 25 square miles in northwest Harris County, around Jersey Village, Willowbrook and a stretch of FM 1960. It was miles away from downtown Houston.

The new geofence turns that zone into the nose cone and adds a long body running southeast to downtown and Bellaire, where it flares out into fins. Pair that with “Houston, we have liftoff,” a riff on the famous Apollo 13 line, and the theme isn’t subtle.

The problem is that a long rocket drawn diagonally across a map also looks a lot like something else, and Tesla has been here before. In July 2025, less than a month into the Austin pilot, Tesla expanded its Austin geofence into a shape that people immediately compared to a penis. Instead of shrugging it off, Elon Musk leaned in:

Bigger, longer and uncut 😂 https://t.co/VmAfkdndMI

That post got about 30 million views. It didn’t age great either. Days later, Waymo expanded its own Austin territory to 90 square miles and was running roughly 100 times more vehicles than Tesla, as we reported at the time. Tesla still had employees riding in every car back then.

Robotaxi Tracker, which crowdsources sightings of Tesla’s Robotaxi vehicles, now lists Tesla’s Houston service area at about 254 square miles, roughly 10 times the launch zone.

The fleet hasn’t grown nearly as fast. The tracker counts 34 unsupervised Model Ys in Houston, up from 22 three months ago, and no Cybercabs yet. That works out to about one car for every 7.5 square miles.

That’s the same pattern we saw in Austin earlier this year, when Tesla expanded “Robotaxi” to the entire Austin metro with only ~20 vehicles. To be fair, Austin has since caught up: the tracker now counts 397 driverless vehicles there, more than half of them Cybercabs.

Waymo has run fully driverless rides there since February and roughly doubled its Houston service area to nearly 50 square miles in May, ahead of the World Cup.

it's just to signal that Tesla and SpaceX plan to merge. Same with the roadster event - it has nothing to do with an actual product. the roadster 2 is vaporware. The event's only purpose is to signal a merger. that's what this map is

Why? There’s no logic in making the service area in this shape. Why not just make it where you think it is safe and useful to operate? All for a joke? It makes no sense to me.

During Tesla’s earnings call last week, the company again made it clear that safety is its biggest bottleneck for Robotaxi expansion. Tesla still limits itself to lower-speed roads and avoids more complex environments; even train crossings have been an issue.

Because of that, geo-fenced areas only tell part of the story. You can now order a ride within this area, but that doesn’t mean Tesla can operate efficiently there.It is limited by the number of vehicles in operation at any given time and the routes those vehicles take. Otherwise, Tesla would have bigger exposure to potential crashes.

#5130. Gnosis Pay data breach exposes customer info; funds unaffected

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach after unauthorized access to its legacy debit card system on Nov. 3. Funds were unaffected; 3D Secure is now mandatory.

Gnosis Pay disclosed a data breach affecting its legacy system powering Gnosis Pay-branded debit cards, exposing certain customer information after unauthorized access was detected on Nov. 3.

The company said it contained the incident within two hours, replaced compromised security credentials and addressed the vulnerability, adding that customer funds were not affected and its enterprise platform powering white-label card programs was untouched.

Gnosis Pay has made 3D Secure verification mandatory for online card payments until the affected system is retired on Dec. 20.

Crypto card providers increasingly rely on third-party infrastructure and legacy systems that can create security vulnerabilities. In recent months, there have been breaches exposing cardholder information and exploits affecting funds held in card accounts.

Based said an attacker accessed an internal dashboard used to manage its Visa card program on Oct. 5. Some customer data was exposed, but the attacker reportedly failed to access deposited funds. In August, a vulnerability in Rain's Solana card infrastructure allowed unauthorized withdrawals from card balances belonging to users of Tria and Avici.

Meanwhile, Revolut said an unauthorized third party used a fraudulent request from a legitimate government email domain to obtain sensitive customer information, potentially including account statements, identity documents and Bitcoin transaction histories. Customer funds and systems were unaffected.

#5129. Europe’s largest asset manager Amundi tokenizes equity index fund on Ethereum

The new share class offers blockchain-based exposure to an index tracking 600 companies across European markets.

Amundi, Europe’s largest fund manager by assets, has launched a tokenized share class of its STOXX Europe 600 equity index fund on Ethereum, expanding its blockchain-based investment offerings beyond money market funds.

The new share class was issued on Ethereum in collaboration with CACEIS, a European asset-servicing company that provided the tokenization infrastructure. The fund will continue to be available through traditional investment channels, Amundi said in a Thursday announcement.

The fund tracks the STOXX Europe 600 Index, which covers 600 large-, mid- and small-cap companies across developed European markets. Amundi said it plans to develop a broader range of tokenized funds for institutional clients and digital platforms, citing potential improvements in accessibility, settlement speed and operational efficiency.

The launch follows the asset manager’s introduction of its first tokenized money market fund share class in November 2025, also developed with CACEIS on Ethereum.

The platform’s 10 largest tokenized equity products by value were all tied to US companies or US-listed securities, including Securitize, SpaceX, Circle and Alphabet.

Meanwhile, European financial firms have pressed regulators to accelerate reforms. In April, a coalition of 39 companies and industry groups warned that the EU risked falling behind the US in tokenized finance, calling for expanded asset eligibility, higher volume limits and the removal of time restrictions on licenses under the bloc’s Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) Pilot Regime.

#5127. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

#5125. US Treasury walks back crypto surveillance ‘travel rule’ proposal

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

FinCEN and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on funds worth $250 or more.

The Treasury Department has withdrawn a controversial proposal that would have expanded reporting requirements for cryptocurrency transactions.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network and the Federal Reserve Board of Governors rescinded the "Travel Rule," which was first proposed in 2020 to require financial institutions to collect information on transactions of $250 or more, decreasing that threshold from $3,000.

In a filing, FinCEN said it was withdrawing the proposed rule because more than five years had passed, the convertible virtual currency industry had changed and additional public input would be helpful.

"The Agencies believe that any future, potential amendments to the Recordkeeping Rule and Travel Rule related to CVC should be considered in light of these recent developments to ensure that such amendments are fit for purpose," FinCEN said.

When the rule was proposed, FinCEN had argued that bad actors were increasingly using smaller transactions to move illicit funds. However, crypto groups, like Coin Center, had warned that lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 would mean that more ordinary transactions would be subject to "warrantless financial surveillance."

In a blog on Friday, Coin Center said it was happy to see the rule proposal rescinded, along with another rule proposal that was withdrawn last month that would have imposed reporting and recordkeeping requirements on transactions involving self-hosted wallets.

"This follows Treasury’s withdrawal last month of the proposed 'unhosted wallet' and cryptocurrency mixing rules, both of which Coin Center had also opposed," the organization said. 'Taken together, these withdrawals close the books on several long-pending proposals that would have expanded the Bank Secrecy Act’s already extensive financial surveillance regime."

Crypto Council for Innovation CEO Ji Hun Kim said in a statement that withdrawing the travel rule was the "right call."

"It had not moved forward over the past several years for good reason," he said. "Lowering the threshold from $3,000 to $250 for cross-border transfers would have placed undue burden on institutions."

#5123. Fed, OCC check US banks after Binance hack as experts flag security risks

US regulators reportedly contacted banks following last week’s $6.3 billion Binance security hack, while experts raised concerns about transaction security and crypto risks.

US regulators reportedly contacted banks following last week’s $6.3 billion Binance hack, as cybersecurity experts raised concerns about how financial institutions handle crypto-related security risks.

Officials from the Federal Reserve and Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (OCC) began contacting banks on Monday to ask about “unusual activity” or service disruptions, Reuters reported Thursday, citing people familiar with the matter.

The reported inquiries could be followed by closer attention to how banks manage crypto-related risks, including custody, counterparty exposure and incident response, according to Stefan Muehlbauer, head of US government affairs at CertiK.

“After an incident of this scale, it would be surprising if the Fed and OCC didn’t check in with the banks they supervise,” Muehlbauer told Cointelegraph. “Their mandate is to protect the financial system, and that means understanding where exposures sit and how quickly they can respond.”

According to Reuters, banks have been assessing the hack and checking their systems to figure out whether they could absorb or manage similar threats.

Reuters’ report also noted that a cybersecurity team at one of the largest US banks contacted Binance within hours of the hack. A Binance spokesperson confirmed with Cointelegraph that the exchange shared its publicly released incident report with all its banking partners and was not aware of any vulnerabilities affecting them.

Muehlbauer explained the procedure currently unfolding is “how a maturing framework should work.” He added that “it’s not a signal that regulators are pulling back from the industry.”

The inquiries also come after the Federal Reserve removed a requirement in April 2025 for state member banks to notify regulators before engaging in crypto activities. The Fed said it would continue overseeing those activities through its regular supervisory process.

A Federal Reserve spokesperson told Cointelegraph that the agency had nothing to share regarding the reported inquiries. Cointelegraph also contacted the OCC for comment but has not heard back at the time of publishing.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Binance said in its Nov. 3 incident report that approximately $6.34 billion in digital assets were affected by the attack on Oct. 29, with $2.85 billion frozen or intercepted as of the report.

According to the crypto exchange, the attack began with compromised software installed by an engineer in June. Hackers later employed social engineering, stolen credentials and malicious software to get access to wallet systems and carry out unauthorized transfers.

Dr. Darren Williams, founder and CEO of cybersecurity firm BlackFog, told Cointelegraph that the methods used in the attack pose potential risks to traditional banks, particularly those offering crypto custody and payment services.

“Attackers no longer need to break encryption or steal private keys if they can compromise the trusted systems and people authorized to move money,” Williams said.

He warned that hackers could use compromised employee devices, accounts or third-party services to move money without triggering any alerts. He also said the growing use of agentic AI in financial operations could pose even more risks.

“The priority must be stopping unauthorized transactions and data exfiltration before assets or sensitive information leave the organization, not simply investigating the damage afterwards,” Williams said.

Binance has since said that it has strengthened its security controls and was bringing in independent cybersecurity experts to review the attack.

#5120. Treasury cuts off MEXC Global over alleged $16 billion in transactions linked to Iran, North Korea and Russia

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

The U.S. Treasury Department says it has severed crypto exchange MEXC Global from the United States after it allegedly supported $16 billion in transactions connected to Iran, North Korea, and Russia.

On Friday, the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network issued an order against MEXC Global and said it was a money laundering concern.

"Financial institutions cannot have it both ways: Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system," said Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent in a statement. "Treasury will continue to sever the Iranian regime’s economic lifelines and thwart its desperate attempts to circumvent U.S. sanctions using digital assets."

Over the past year, Treasury has implemented a crackdown of sorts on crypto platforms allegedly helping Iran evade sanctions amid its ongoing war against the country. In June, Iran's largest crypto exchange, Nobitex, was sanctioned after Treasury said it processed more than half of Iran's crypto inflows in 2025. In September, the U.S. also sanctioned Iranian crypto exchange BitBank over alleged bitcoin transfers to the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps.

On Friday, Treasury said that though MEXC Global said it maintained anti-money laundering and says it will block the use of people using it in countries including Iran and North Korea, that it had "severe deficiencies" in those AML controls. The department said Friday's sanction will protect the U.S. financial system.

"Today's action will protect the U.S. financial system from the significant illicit finance threats posed by MEXC Global," the department said.

#5119. Binance, Deribit revoke SignalPlus API keys after fake Teams invite attack

SignalPlus said attackers compromised an engineer’s computer in a breach that resulted in losses of less than $82,000, which the crypto trading platform pledged to reimburse in full.

Exchanges Binance and Deribit said Thursday they had revoked API keys connected to crypto derivatives trading platform SignalPlus as a precaution following a security breach. Both exchanges said their own systems were unaffected, while Deribit warned that trading, monitoring and automation through SignalPlus had stopped working for its customers.

Binance co-founder Yi He also urged third-party platforms integrated with the exchange to review their security controls and monitor for suspicious activity, warning that the threat extended beyond any single company.

SignalPlus provides trading infrastructure for crypto derivatives, particularly options, allowing professional and institutional traders to manage positions across multiple exchanges through a single platform.

According to the company on X, the attack originated from a compromised Telegram account belonging to a contact at a third-party technology vendor. The attacker impersonated the contact and sent a fake Microsoft Teams meeting invitation to a SignalPlus engineer, compromising the employee’s computer.

The attacker remained dormant on the compromised computer before using the engineer’s access privileges to target SignalPlus’s internal network early Wednesday, the company said.

SignalPlus said it detected and contained the intrusion, with total losses of less than $82,000, and had begun fully reimbursing affected users. The company temporarily suspended its services and expects to restore operations over the next few days after completing security reviews and applying patches.

SignalPlus also acknowledged that it had not required IP whitelisting for exchange API access. “We take responsibility for this oversight,” the company said, adding that it would tighten API access requirements and strengthen internal security controls. IP whitelisting is a security measure that restricts API access to a predefined list of approved internet addresses.

Related: BitMart founder says security team ‘severely understaffed’ after new hack

The SignalPlus incident comes days after Binance disclosed details of a $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29, which also involved compromised employee computers.

According to Binance’s postmortem, attackers posing as recruiters tricked an engineer into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The attackers subsequently used the compromised computer to gain access to additional systems and eventually breach the exchange’s wallet infrastructure.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and attempts to steal employee credentials.

#5117. Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute: Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

📉 Bitcoin Erases Election Rally as AI Stocks Crater

On Wednesday, the market decided a Democratic Congress was good news. Stocks had their best day in three months, Bitcoin climbed back over $80,800, and Trump posted on Truth Social that the market “just hit another Record High” with 401(k)s “through the roof.”

On Thursday, the market changed its mind.

The S&P 500 fell 4.36%, its worst session since the Liberation Day tariffs in April 2025. The Nasdaq dropped 5.52% and closed in correction, more than 10% below its record from last month. The Dow shed 1,833 points.

The damage concentrated in one place. The iShares Semiconductor ETF fell more than 12%, its worst day since March 2020. Micron lost 17.5%, AMD 17.3%, Marvell 16.4%, Nvidia 9.3%. The Magnificent Seven shed roughly $1 trillion in market value. The VIX touched 28.09 intraday, its highest since March. By the close, the U.S. stock market had lost $3T in value - more than the entire market cap of crypto.

Notably, the thing that spooked investors was the same election result that lifted everything a day earlier. Several states where Democrats won have active local opposition to data center construction, and the market spent Thursday pricing in slower permitting and tighter oversight of the AI buildout. Ipek Ozkardeskaya at Swissquote put it simply: “We suspect there is going to be some scrutiny on this aggressive AI buildout.” Rate expectations moved too, with December hike odds falling to 70.1% from 82.7%.

Crypto did not sit this one out. Bitcoin fell 5.4% to $75,871, below where it traded before the election. Ethereum dropped 7.6% to $2,239, a steeper fall than the Nasdaq. XRP lost 9.8%, and Hyperliquid fell 4.9%. The CMC20 index fell 6.29%. Almost $1.1B in crypto longs were liquidated on the day, before a slight relief rally overnight.

Friday’s October jobs report is next. After a week where the market read the same political outcome two opposite ways inside two sessions, it is fair to wonder how much anyone actually knows right now. We will find out soon…

#5115. Crypto ally French Hill wins reelection but set to lose House chair

Republican French Hill secured another term in Congress but will lose his leadership of the House Financial Services Committee when Democrats take control in January.

House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill, a key Republican ally of the cryptocurrency industry, has won reelection in Arkansas but is expected to lose his committee chairmanship after Democrats won control of the House of Representatives in Tuesday’s midterm elections.

The Associated Press called the race Thursday, confirming Hill’s victory over Democratic challenger Chris Jones in Arkansas’ 2nd Congressional District. Hill, who sponsored the CLARITY Act and voted for the GENIUS Act, holds an A rating from the crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, which classifies him as strongly supportive of cryptocurrency.

Fairshake, a cryptocurrency industry-backed super political action committee (PAC), pledged $1 million to support Hill’s reelection campaign in October as part of its efforts to retain crypto-friendly lawmakers in Congress.

California Democrat Maxine Waters, the current ranking member of the House Financial Services Committee, is the leading contender to replace Hill as chair, according to Reuters reporting.

Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress in Tuesday’s midterm elections, raising uncertainty over the future of US crypto legislation and the prospect of increased industry scrutiny. Crypto-backed super PACs spent more than $176 million during the election cycle.

#5114. BMW’s new iX5 looks like a big upgrade next to the old X5 [Images]

BMW’s new iX5 is hard to miss with its distinct Double-X headlights. Here’s how it stacks up against the outgoing X5, side by side.

The X5 is among BMW’s best-selling vehicles in the US. In its fifth generation, it will be available across five powertrains.

In the US, it will be offered in gas, plug-in hybrid (PHEV), and, for the first time, battery electric (EV) powertrains. Other markets will also see diesel and hydrogen fuel cell variants.

The gas-powered 2027 X5 40 xDrive is now available, starting at $72,100, but the electric iX5 won’t go on sale until November.

Ahead of its official launch next month, Bimmertoday posted several images of the new iX5 parked next to the outgoing X5 G05, giving us a good side-by-side comparison.

Although it still looks like an X5, the new iX5 stands apart with BMW’s Neue Klasse design. The new upright, slimmer BMW Kidney Grille replaces the old wide, chunkier grille design.

A post shared by bimmertoday (@bimmertoday)

You can also see BMW’s new “double-X” light signature, which combines the headlights, daytime running lights, side lights, and turn signals into one unit. The Double-X headlights come standard, but BMW said you can turn them off for simpler diagonal stripes.

The new BMW iX5 60 xDrive will go on sale in the US next month. Using a 144 kWh battery, BMW said the electric SUV will offer up to 435 miles of range.

The iX5 is powered by a dual-motor all-wheel-drive powertrain, delivering a combined 578 hp (425 kW) and 593 lb-ft of torque. BMW says it can go from 0 to 60 mph in 4.4 seconds and tow up to 6,000 lbs.

BMW’s new iX5 is 197.2″ long, 78.7″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, or about the size of the Porsche Cayenne Electric.
