#5088. Musk declares Tesla Robotaxi win over Waymo, but Waymo still drives 7x more

Elon Musk says Tesla’s Robotaxi race against Waymo “was never a real contest” after Tesla’s driverless fleet logged 438,000 miles in Austin in a single week, edging past Waymo’s 406,000 weekly average in the city.

That’s one city, one week, and a Waymo figure that’s four months old. Across the US, Waymo still drives about seven times more driverless miles than Tesla every week.

The chart going around comes from Robotaxi Tracker, a community project that pulls driverless mileage from each company’s own reports: Tesla’s weekly Robotaxi safety report and Waymo’s quarterly Safety Impact data hub.

Jason Scharf posted the Austin view on X with the comment that “the battle for Autonomous Austin might already be over.” Musk replied overnight: “It was never a real contest.”

The Austin data is real –albeit with some caveats. In the week ending October 18, Tesla reported 438,000 miles with no safety monitor aboard in Austin, up 30% from the week before. Waymo averaged about 406,000 miles a week in Austin in the second quarter.

But the tracker’s own caption explains the catch. Tesla’s figure is its latest week. Waymo’s is an average of April through June, because Waymo publishes about three months after each quarter ends. So Musk is comparing Tesla’s best week ever to Waymo’s spring.

Switch the same chart to “All markets” and the story flips.

Waymo averaged 3.90 million rider-only miles per week in Q2 2026. Tesla did 558,000 miles in its best week. Waymo’s Phoenix, Bay Area, and Los Angeles markets each drive more miles per week on their own than Tesla’s entire network.

Cumulative numbers are even more lopsided. Waymo has logged 271.3 million rider-only miles since 2020, including 50.7 million in Q2 alone, as we covered when Waymo released its latest safety data. Tesla has 3.11 million driverless miles since January 22, 2026. Waymo drove more than that in a single week.

And Tesla’s total leans almost entirely on one city:

Austin accounts for 79% of Tesla’s weekly driverless miles. Houston, Tampa, Orlando, and Miami combined did about 25,000 miles. Waymo’s smallest tracked market, Atlanta, averaged 250,000 a week in Q2.

The Austin surge has an obvious cause. Tesla launched public Cybercab rides in Austin on September 3.

According to Robotaxi Tracker’s count of the Texas DMV automated-vehicle list, Tesla now has 463 Cybercabs registered in Texas, 337 of them added in the last 30 days. That’s 48% of the 970 autonomous vehicles Tesla has registered in the state.

Tesla isn’t hiding the strategy. On the Q3 earnings call last week, Ashok Elluswamy, Tesla’s head of AI software, said the company wants to “swarm Austin with Cybercabs everywhere.” Musk said the Austin Cybercab fleet had grown “by a factor of 10” in about a month.

So Tesla dumped hundreds of new, company-owned cars into one metro and the miles went up. That’s fleet deployment. It doesn’t show the software can do what Waymo does in 15 cities, and Tesla can’t repeat it everywhere at once.

The bigger problem is what the cars are allowed to do.

On the same earnings call, Elluswamy described the service as constrained by the software, not by regulators. He said that if there’s “the slightest possible risk at all,” Tesla doesn’t take it on, and that the limits are “technology driven” for now. Service hours only recently stretched from 9 p.m. toward 2 a.m., and he listed highways, “higher speed roads,” 24/7 operation, and more weather conditions as things Tesla still has to unlock.

You can see those limits in the rides. Tesla Robotaxis don’t take highways, and riders report they avoid at-grade railroad crossings. In September, a rider had a 10-minute Cybercab trip in Austin turn into a 70-minute detour because the car drove south through East Austin until it found a bridge over the rail line, instead of taking MoPac.

That matters for this comparison, too. Miles is a flattering metric when your car takes the long way. A trip that should cover a few miles and instead loops across town logs a lot more mileage for the same rider.

Waymo, meanwhile, has been taking riders on freeways in Phoenix, San Francisco, and Los Angeles since November 2025.

Credit where it’s due: Tesla’s driverless miles are growing fast, 30% in a single week, and Austin now has a real Tesla fleet carrying the public without anyone in the driver’s seat. A year ago, that wasn’t the case.

I continue to hear tesla refer to its miles as 'driverless miles', but Waymo's as rider only miles. This can be a big difference. Are you sure that this is not an apples and oranges comparison? Do we have confidence that Tesla is only including miles with a rider inside and not driverless miles where the can is spinning circles in a parking lot??

But “never a real contest” is a strange thing to say when you’re behind by 7x on weekly miles and 87x on cumulative miles, and your one win depends on comparing your newest week to your rival’s data from last spring.

What stands out to us is the type of autonomy Tesla is deploying. In 2020, Musk wrote that “Waymo is impressive, but a highly specialized solution,” and that Tesla’s approach was “a general solution.” For years, Tesla fans dismissed Waymo as a geofenced system that only worked in mapped areas with lots of restrictions.

Now look at Tesla’s Robotaxi: geofenced, no highways, avoiding railroad crossings, taking 70 minutes for 10-minute trips, and limited by hours because the software isn’t ready for anything with “the slightest possible risk.” By Musk’s own old definition, that’s a highly specialized solution. Waymo is the one driving on freeways now.

Robotaxis still need electricity, and so does your EV. If you drive electric, pairing it with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. It’s a free service with hundreds of pre-vetted installers competing for your business, so you save 20 to 30% compared to going it alone. No sales calls until you pick an installer. #5087. Muhammad Ali at Home: 10 Photos of the Heavyweight Champion’s Life Outside the Ring

It was a stroke of luck—bad luck—that got Muhammad Ali into the ring for the first time. The Louisville, Kentucky, native was 12 years old when his brand-new bicycle was stolen. The police officer to whom Ali reported the theft happened to own a boxing gym—and became his first coach.

Ten years later, in 1964, Ali, then still known by his birth name, Cassius Clay, became the world heavyweight champion when he defeated reigning titleholder Sonny Liston. The athlete, who earned the nickname “The Greatest,” would go on to win that title two more times, as well as an Olympic gold medal and numerous honors for his civil rights activism and humanitarian work.

There was, of course, another side to the legend: his domestic life. As we gear up for the release of the Prime Video series The Greatest, which explores the prizefighter’s rise to prominence, we’re looking back at photos of Ali in his off-duty hours.

Days after becoming the heavyweight champion, Ali announced his conversion to the Nation of Islam, changed his name, and then relocated to Chicago, where the controversial religion’s headquarters are based. (He later converted to Sunni Islam). In 1967, Ali cited his religious beliefs when he refused to serve in the US Army during the Vietnam War. His boxing license revoked and his championship title stripped, Ali entered a period of exile. He is pictured in his Chicago home the following year, reading a book by Nation of Islam leader Elijah Muhammad. The athlete shared the dwelling with his second wife, Khalilah Camacho-Ali, whom he married at the residence on August 17, 1967. The single-story brick house was in the Avalon Park neighborhood and was a fully furnished gift from the Nation of Islam.

The boxer and his toddler Maryum feeding 10-week-old twins Rasheda and Jamillah in their Philadelphia home.

The couple welcomed a daughter, Maryum, in 1968, and two years later, twins named Rasheda and Jamillah. The newborns are pictured between Ali and Maryum at home in Pennsylvania in this November 1970 photograph. The fighter and his family stayed at the home, in West Philadelphia’s Overbrook Park neighborhood, for about a year. It was later owned by Kobe Bryant’s grandparents.

Ali with fans at his Pennsylvania training camp in August 1974.

Though he soon relocated to New Jersey, Ali would maintain ties to Pennsylvania for decades with his training camp, Fighter’s Heaven. He purchased the Deer Lake property in 1972, the year after the Supreme Court overturned his draft-evasion conviction, allowing him to regain his boxing license. On the land, he custom-built a log gym, a dining hall, several cabins, a mosque, and a courtyard with a large stone fireplace. His training sessions were open to the public; in this August 1974 photo, a group of fans are pictured behind him at the camp. He kept the retreat long after his retirement, selling it in 1997 to martial arts instructor George Dillman. The property is now a tourist destination, complete with a small museum.

Ali at home in Los Angeles before his fight with Larry Holmes.

Ali briefly retired from boxing at age 37 in 1979, but returned the following year to fight Larry Holmes. The boxer poses at his home office in Hancock Park, Los Angeles, in this August 1980 photo, taken about a month before his fight with Holmes.

Ali shoots pool in his Hancock Park house in this 1980 photo.

Ali shows off the game room in his Hancock Park, Los Angeles, home in this 1980 snapshot. Around this period, he started to show early symptoms of Parkinson’s disease, which impacted his athletic ability. He lost the fight to Holmes and went back into retirement.

The boxing champion and his wife Veronica at home in 1982.

In 1981, Ali fought one last time before hanging up his gloves. “I know it’s the end,” he said after losing the fight. “I’m not crazy. After Holmes, I had excuses. I was too light. Didn’t breathe right. No excuses this time. I’m happy. I’m still pretty. I could have a black eye. Broken teeth. Split lips. I think I came out all right for an old man.” Ali stands next to his third wife, Veronica Porché, in front of his Hancock Park dwelling in this photo, taken a few months after his final match.

The Greatest takes a call at home in 1982.

In this photo from the same March 1982 photo shoot, Ali strikes a pose in his opulent Los Angeles living room. The retired boxer had purchased the 14,500-square-foot home in 1979, two years after marrying Porché. The 1916 build, by architect John C. Austin, was an Italian Renaissance mansion complete with seven bedrooms.

The estate featured stained glass window panels designed by Louis Comfort Tiffany (the son of Tiffany & Co. founder Charles Lewis Tiffany) and a stately dining room with intricately carved dark wood and a crystal chandelier, as seen in this October 1985 photo of Ali eating cereal. That year, he and Porché filed for divorce.

Ali outfitted the home with a (packed) trophy room, which he shows off in this 1985 picture. He left the Hancock Park dwelling the following year, when his and Porché’s divorce was finalized. Shortly after, Ali married his fourth wife, Lonnie Williams. As his Parkinson’s disease progressed (he was officially diagnosed in 1984), Williams became Ali’s primary caregiver.

The former fighter has his picture taken by photographer Neil Leifer at home in Arizona’s Paradise Valley.

Ali poses for a photo shoot commemorating his 70th birthday in 2012 at the Arizona home he and Williams shared. That year, he was honored with the Liberty Medal for his humanitarian efforts, which he devoted more time to following his retirement. He was also awarded the Presidential Medal of Freedom in 2005. Ali died in Arizona in 2016 at 74.

#5086. What You Should Know About Exercising When Bone Density Is a Concern

Osteopenia or osteoporosis doesn’t have to sideline your workouts. Here’s what to do, how to modify, and when to back off.

You might have heard that being active is a good idea when you have osteopenia or osteoporosis, conditions marked by lower-than-normal bone density. But when you’re exercising specifically to improve bone health, some types of exercise may be more helpful than others. Before lacing up your sneakers, there are a few things to know.

“When patients ask me what they can do to preserve and protect their bone health, consistent weight bearing and resistance exercises are the first things I recommend,” says Nicole Leung, MD, director of the Osteoporosis and Bone Health Program at NYU Langone Health in New York City. “Bone is a living tissue, so it adapts to mechanical load through remodeling.”

When bone tissue is stressed via physical activity, your body responds by making more bone, helping strengthen the tissue. Exercise also lowers the activity of cells that dissolve and break down old bone. That combo helps preserve or even improve your bone mineral density, regardless of your health status. Exercise may slow or even prevent the progression of osteoporosis; if you have osteoporosis already, it can help you maintain the bone you have and reduce your risk for falls and fractures.

A bone-density diagnosis doesn’t mean you have to give up exercise—but your routine should reflect your fracture risk and health history. Here’s what Dr. Leung recommends for choosing activities, adapting your routine, and knowing when to check with your doctor before making major changes.

There are a few types of activities that make up a bone-friendly exercise plan, and it’s important to get the right amount of each. Here are the types of exercise to do regularly.

These are exercises where you’re on your feet and putting force on your muscles and bones as they work against gravity. Depending on factors like your age, fitness level, and bone mineral density, these activities can include:

People working to maintain their bone health should do these sorts of activities for 30 to 60 minutes, three to five days per week, according to the American College of Sports Medicine.

As for activities like swimming and cycling, they’re great for getting your heart pumping—but because they’re low-impact, they won’t do much for your bones. That said, feel free to do them in addition to your weight-bearing exercises if you enjoy them.

Lifting (relatively) heavy things works your muscles and helps build bone density. This includes free weights, machines, resistance bands, or body weight exercises like squats or push-ups. Aim for two to three strength sessions per week, and don’t be afraid to gradually go heavier (with your doctor’s okay). Pushing yourself to 70 to 90 percent of your max effort may deliver a bigger bone boost than lifting lighter weights, research suggests.

Having osteoporosis makes you more prone to fractures after falls, but you can strengthen your balance—and reduce your chances for a broken bone—with exercises like yoga, tai chi, or Pilates. “Any exercise where you have to shift your body weight side to side and forward and back can be very helpful for your stabilizing muscles,” Dr. Leung says. She recommends working balance exercises into your schedule a few times per week. Find safe modifications if you’re worried about falling, like holding onto a sturdy chair or touching a wall.

Whether you’ve been exercising for years, are just starting out, or are returning to a routine after a long hiatus, Dr. Leung advises paying attention to your posture, avoiding exercise when you’re overly exhausted, and increasing intensity gradually. Older women who are new to exercise can still do resistance training, she says, but may need to start at a slower pace and under supervision.

Despite the overall benefits of exercise, you still need to be careful, particularly when you have osteoporosis. Certain activities can set the stage for potential spine fractures, so before starting an exercise program, decide with your doctor or other qualified professional what is and isn’t safe for you.

According to the International Osteoporosis Foundation, you may need to limit or avoid moves that involve:

Yoga, while helpful for balance, can involve poses that put your spine at risk (like forward bends, deep twists, plow pose, shoulder stand, and pigeon pose). If you decide to give it a try, choose a class where the teacher can offer safe modifications, Dr. Leung says. It’s also best to steer clear of activities that come with a high risk of falling from above standing height, like skiing or horseback riding, she adds.

When it comes to stressing your bones, it’s possible to get too much of a good thing. “Overburdening your bone can actually lead to stress fractures,” Dr. Leung says. If you do prolonged intense weight-bearing and high-impact exercises like jogging or jumping rope, avoid scheduling them on back-to-back days. Alternate those workouts with activities like strength training, or have an active recovery day where you walk or do yoga or tai chi, she recommends.

Make it a point to stay active even after you’ve completed the day’s workout. According to Dr. Leung, non-exercise activities like casual walking or doing chores may help to maintain bone density when compared to sedentary behavior. Stroll over to chat with a coworker at their desk during a break, take the stairs when you can, or walk while you call a friend instead of sitting. A few minutes here and there can really add up.

Any new or worsening bone or spine pain is a warning sign to stop exercising and contact your doctor. You should also call your doctor if you fall while exercising, notice that you’ve had height loss, or if your spine appears curved, which could be signs of a spinal fracture. And always stop exercising and seek medical attention if you experience other severe symptoms like dizziness, faintness, or chest pain.

Consistency counts. People with low bone density who stay active over several months tend to see the greatest bone-health benefits, studies show. Choose activities you enjoy, build up gradually, and find a rhythm you can sustain. The goal isn’t to nail every workout—it’s to keep movement in your life.

#5085. The 2027 Kia EV9 costs $90 more, but at least you get a few extras

Kia barely touched the 2027 EV9’s price, raising it by only $90 while sneaking in a few extra standard features on select trims.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV returns for the 2027 model year, mostly as a carryover model with a few design and packaging updates.

The 2027 Kia EV9 starts at $54,990, up a measly $90 from last year’s model. That’s for the base EV9 Light short-range trim, which uses a 76.1 kWh battery and delivers up to 230 miles of range.

Higher-priced trims, including the Light Long Range, Wind, Land, and GT-Line, use a larger 99.8 kWh battery that delivers up to 305 miles of range in RWD variants.

The Wind and Land trims have standard all-wheel-drive (AWD) and offer up to 283 miles of range, while the GT-Line is also AWD but offers slightly less range at 280 miles.

Kia’s dual-motor e-AWD system delivers up to 379 hp and 516 lb-ft of torque in the EV9 GT-Line, enabling it to accelerate from 0 to 60 mph in 4.5 seconds.

For the 2027 model, the EV9 Wind now comes with black-finish 19″ wheels and the Glossy Black exterior paint color as standard, and Wolf Gray paint is newly available as an option.

The 2027 EV9 Land now includes the Nightfall Edition Package, which was previously optional, as standard. It starts at $69,990, or $500 less than the 2026 Kia EV9 Land Nightfall Edition.

The package includes a considerable performance boost with torque rising from 379 lb-ft to 516 lb-ft, plus an exclusive black interior and exterior design elements.

Inside, the 2027 EV9 features Kia’s Digital Cockpit with nearly 30″ of combined displays. The setup includes dual 12.3″ instrument displays and navigation screens in a curved panoramic display, plus a 5″ climate control screen.

Kia’s three-row electric SUV offers up to 42.8″ of legroom in the second row, which is even more than the 2025 Cadillac Escalade. It’s available in six- and seven-seat configurations.

The EV9 is 197.2″ long, 77.9″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, with a 122″ wheelbase, or slightly smaller than the Rivian R1S and Hyundai IONIQ 9.

With DC fast charging of up to 350 kW, the 2027 Kia EV9’s battery can charge from 10% to 80% in as little as 24 minutes.

All 2027 Kia EV9s also come with a built-in NACS charge port, so you can use Tesla Superchargers without a separate adaptor.

#5084. Chainalysis says breach exposed password hashes and live tokens

Chainalysis disclosed password hashes and live tokens were exposed after initially reporting only non-sensitive customer data was accessed.

Chainalysis on Wednesday disclosed a wider impact from a cyberattack it initially described as involving only non-sensitive customer information.

“On Monday, we discovered certain customer information including names, usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all Chainalysis users were exposed to a malicious actor,” the blockchain analytics firm said in an update to its incident notice.

The company did not disclose whether each type of information was exposed for every user and declined to provide Cointelegraph with additional information beyond its published disclosure.

Chainalysis initially said on Oct. 30 that the intrusion exposed user IDs, names and email addresses but found no evidence that attackers accessed authentication credentials or session tokens.

Another Wednesday update stated an “on-path attack” exposed some customers’ passwords. The company said it had contained and remediated that attack vector and was locking affected accounts and requiring password resets.

Despite the broader exposure, Chainalysis said it still had no evidence that attackers accessed investigation data or manipulated its attribution data.

#5083. Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

Bitcoin ETFs Bleed $430 Million as Tech Rout Tests Wall Street Buyers

U.S. spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds lost $430.2 million to net withdrawals Thursday as a technology-stock selloff spread to crypto, with Fidelity and BlackRock leading the exits.

Fidelity's Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC) posted $199.9 million in net outflows and BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) shed $105 million, according to Farside Investors. Together they accounted for about 71% of the day's withdrawals.

Grayscale's GBTC lost another $49.9 million, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC shed $32.9 million. No fund in Farside's table recorded positive net flows Thursday, although several reported no net movement.

The latest withdrawals brought this week's net outflow to $869.8 million, following a $643.2 million outflow last Friday. Wednesday's modest $74.6 million inflow offered a brief reprieve before Thursday's reversal.

Fidelity's swing was particularly sharp: FBTC attracted $236.7 million Wednesday, then surrendered nearly $200 million the next session. BlackRock, meanwhile, lost $201.2 million even on Wednesday's positive day, extending a five-session withdrawal streak since October 30 to roughly $1.04 billion.

ETF flows have often been treated as a gauge of demand from investors accessing Bitcoin through regular brokerage accounts. The latest figures show that access works both ways: the same familiar wrapper that makes buying easy also makes exiting straightforward.

Because ETFs trade on stock exchanges, investors can adjust their Bitcoin exposure without transferring coins to a crypto wallet. That convenience can also make the funds an easy place to cut risk during an equity selloff. Daily net flows capture shares created or redeemed at the fund level, not every trade between investors.

Thursday's redemptions arrived as the Nasdaq Composite plunged 5.5% and the S&P 500 fell about 4.4%. Chipmakers and data-center stocks led the decline as investors questioned the pace of artificial-intelligence spending and potential restrictions after the U.S. midterm elections.

Bitcoin also surrendered its post-election rebound, falling back toward $76,000 after trading above $80,000 Wednesday. Short liquidations had helped power that rally, making the subsequent reversal more painful for leveraged traders.

The ETF figures add another piece to that picture. Net flows measure creations minus redemptions of ETF shares; they cannot tell whether holders were raising cash to cover losses elsewhere, trimming Bitcoin exposure, or rebalancing portfolios.
